===== page 1
NUS-H2: First-Principles Derivations
Nuclear-Upgraded Steam Methane Reforming with Carbon Capture
for Low-Carbon Hydrogen in Singapore
Wee Zhern Ray
MIT licence. OUTRAM PARK (github.com/theodoreOnzGit/outram-park-backend) was consulted
as a reference; no OUTRAM PARK code is included and no endorsement is implied.
Every number on these pages comes from the tools script. Of its 619 numbers, 360 are read
from the results files, 109 are computed from results values, 139 are computed here from
published inputs, 3 are published and 2 are assumed. Five values of the Case A sizing walk are
read from the design decisions table and the low reactor corner of 163.0 is taken from the
screening table, because no results row holds them. The 83 validation status lines are written
from the validation results file.

===== page 3
1
Reference Frame
This section is the language of the whole document. It lists every symbol, every numbered equation
and the assumptions and ranges needed to read them. Sections A to L then take each equation in
turn.
Basis.
Case B is the headline plant: 100,000 Nm3 h−1 of hydrogen, or 66,925 t y−1, with 120.8 MWth
of process heat, 23.2 MMSCFD of natural gas, a reactor rated 210 MWth against 178.5 MWth required,
and 578,052 t y−1 of direct abatement, all on the Headline preset of the final results. Case A, at
130 MMSCFD of hydrogen, is the INL reference. PSA tail gas is recycled across the plant boundary.
SMR means steam methane reforming throughout this document. The unabated SMR is a steam
methane reforming plant with no capture and the same hydrogen output, the comparison case.
How to read an equation card.
Each card gives the number, the statement, a physical inter-
pretation, the working that fits its tier, the variables with SI units, the assumptions, the source, the
implementation reference and the validation method. Implementation references name the engine item
that computes the equation, by crate and module path. A card whose item carries a doc line “eq”
with its number is linked, and a card with no engine item says that it is not implemented. A card
that the engine does not implement may name the item planned for it.
Tier R rigorous, meaning first principles or a published model used inside its stated range. O reduced-
order. E empirical correlation. F fixed design assumption. X external boundary. A published
model used outside its range is tier O and its card says so.
Evidence [P] published, with the page given. [T] textbook result, chapter only, page not checked
because the book is not in the library. [C] calculated here. [A] assumed. [Mn] source missing,
listed in Section M.
Assumption codes SS steady state. 1D axial only. PF plug flow. IG ideal gas. AD adiabatic. CP
constant heat capacity. NKE kinetic and potential energy neglected. IN inert carrier gases in
the solvent.
Validation codes ID analytic identity in a unit test. GOLD agreement with the screening reference
values, which is verification of a port and never validation. PUB comparison with a published
calculated value. EXP comparison with measured data. NONE no benchmark exists, and the
card says so.
Page numbers “PDF p.” is the page in the file. “Printed p.” is the number on the page.
Status of this version.
Every number on these pages comes from the tools script. Of its 619
numbers, 360 are read from the results files, 109 are computed from results values, 139 are computed
here from published inputs, 3 are published and 2 are assumed. Five values of the Case A sizing walk
are read from the design decisions table and the low reactor corner of 163.0 is taken from the screening
table, because no results row holds them. The 83 validation status lines are written from the validation
results file. The engine test suite ran on the engine at commit 4de49a2: 363 tests passed, 0 failed and
0 were ignored. Of these, 109 carry an equation name and 109 of them passed. Of the 299 cards,
265 are linked to the engine: a doc line names the card on an engine item and the implementation
line names that item. Of the rest, 27 are stated as not implemented, 4 are theory and 3 are listed
only. Of the 281 cards with a benchmark code other than NONE, 101 have an engine test named for
them that passed in the recorded run and 180 have none. A validation line that names rows of the
validation results prints the outcome those rows record, so the text follows the results on every build.
A benchmark stated on a card without a results outcome is the target of its named test. Where this
document has computed the comparison itself, the card says “computed here” and gives the result.
2

===== page 4
Symbols that collide in the sources.
R is the gas constant, and reactor rating is ˙Qrated. A is
area, fav availability and A abatement. Every eﬀiciency carries a subscript: ηpc power conversion,
ηpp per-pass capture, ηpl plant capture, ηcat
j
catalyst effectiveness, ηis isentropic, ηm motor. εb is bed
porosity and εHX exchanger effectiveness. α is CO2 loading and h a heat transfer coeﬀicient, although
JAERI writes α for the latter. ϕc is a carbon split and φi a fugacity coeﬀicient. γi is an activity
coeﬀicient and κ = cp/cv.
Units.
SI at every interface.
Kinetics are coded in the published units (bar, kmol, kg of
catalyst, hour) and converted at the function boundary, because the Xu and Froment nota-
tion mixes them.
Conversions used:
1 MMSCFD = 13.83 mol s−1 at 60 ◦F, 1 st = 907.185 kg,
1 MMBtu h−1 = 0.29307 MW.

2
Variables
Each block below expands one row of the seed table: what the quantity means, its typical value, its
units, the job it does in the model and the caveat that goes with it. Symbols that appear in a single
card are defined on that card and listed in the Reference Frame table.
Plant scale and product
Variable: Scale factor (s)
Ratio of the Case B hydrogen mass flow to the INL Case A hydrogen mass flow. Typical value: 0.6833
at Case B and 1.0000 at Case A. Units: Dimensionless. Role: Scales INL stream data and flowsheet
duties to the headline plant size. Caveat: Linear scaling assumes the INL flowsheet keeps its ratios at
the smaller size. Every scaled row says so on its card.
Variable: Hydrogen product rate ( ˙mH2)
Hydrogen leaving the external PSA boundary. Typical value: 2.497 at Case B and 3.654 at Case A.
Units: kg s−1. Role: Sets the size of every downstream ledger. Caveat: Stated at the external PSA
boundary, because the PSA is outside the simulated plant.
Variable: Annual hydrogen (MH2)
Hydrogen sold in a year at the assumed availability. Typical value: 66,925 at Case B and 97,946 at
Case A. Units: t y−1. Role: Denominator of intensity and cost per kilogram. Caveat: Hydrogen runs
only in the availability hours. Backup firing is a sensitivity, so these tonnes carry no firing emissions.
Variable: Availability (fav)
Fraction of the year at rated output.
Typical value: 0.85, with a range of 0.65 to 0.90.
Units:
Dimensionless.
Role: Converts rates to annual totals through th.
Caveat: Assumed.
Published
studies assume 90 to 92%, and no measured HTGR availability was found.
24

===== page 26
Variable: Annual hours (th)
Operating hours in a year, 8760fav. Typical value: 7,446. Units: h y−1. Role: Links hourly flows to
annual ledgers. Caveat: Moves one for one with fav.
Variable: Natural gas rate ( ˙VNG)
Natural gas drawn across the plant boundary. Typical value: 23.2 at Case B and 34.0 at Case A.
Units: MMSCFD, with 1 MMSCFD = 13.83 mol s−1 at 60 ◦F. Role: Sets feed carbon and the gas
bill. Caveat: The Case A value rests on the INL recycle of tail gas. Without recycle the demand rises.
Reactor and helium loops
Variable: Selected rating ( ˙Qrated)
Reactor thermal rating chosen for the plant. Typical value: 210 at Case B. Case A has no selected
rating. Units: MWth. Role: Sets reactor capital in the cost ladder. Caveat: A project choice. The
rating earns no revenue above what the plant draws.
Variable: Reactor heat required ( ˙QR)
Thermal power the plant actually draws from the core, from Eq. B.20. Typical value: 178.5 at Case
B and 260.7 at Case A. Units: MWth. Role: Closes the nuclear island balance. Caveat: Depends on
ηpc, whose net or gross status is open.
Variable: Spare margin ( ˙Qspare)
Rating less requirement, Eq. B.21. Typical value: 31.5 at Case B. At Case A the value is -50.7, so 210
MWth cannot carry that plant. Units: MWth. Role: Shows how much of the rating sits idle. Caveat:
Credited with no revenue.
Variable: Reactor outlet temperature (TROT)
Helium temperature leaving the core. Typical value: 1198.15 (925 C). Units: K. Role: Top of the
temperature cascade in Eq. C.17. Caveat: Taken from the INL Case 6 basis. No HTGR has run a
long campaign at this value with a process heat load.
Variable: Secondary supply temperature (Ts,sup)
Secondary helium leaving the IHX for the reformer. Typical value: 1173.15 (900 C). Units: K. Role:
Hot end of the reformer heat source. Caveat: Assumes a 25 K IHX approach and no duct drop.
Variable: Secondary return temperature (Ts,ret)
Secondary helium returning to the IHX. Typical value: 739.15 (466 C). Units: K. Role: Sets the
secondary flow through Eq. B.8.
Caveat: Published for INL Case 6 at its own preheat train.
A
different train moves it.
25

===== page 27
Variable: Reformer exit temperature (Tref,out)
Process gas temperature at the catalyst exit. Typical value: 1144.15 (871 C). Units: K. Role: Sets
methane conversion through Eq. D.11. Caveat: An INL Gibbs result with no tube model behind it.
Sensitivities at 825 and 800 C are carried.
Variable: Secondary helium flow ( ˙ms)
Mass flow in the intermediate loop. Typical value: 53.6 at Case B on process heat alone, 54.0 with
the slip heat, and 78.49 published at Case A. Units: kg s−1. Role: Sets the secondary circulator and
IHX. Caveat: Published by INL at Case A only.
Variable: Primary helium flow ( ˙mp)
Mass flow through the core at ˙QR. Typical value: 79.2 at Case B and 116 at Case A. Units: kg s−1.
Role: Sets the primary circulator and core rise. Caveat: Calculated. INL publishes no primary flow.
Variable: Process heat ( ˙Qproc)
Heat handed to the process by secondary helium. Typical value: 120.8 at Case B and 176.8 at Case
A. Units: MWth. Role: Largest term in the reactor balance. Caveat: Ahn and Lee print 143.4 MWth
on a comparable duty, so this basis has a stated upper case.
Variable: Slip recycle heat ( ˙Qrec)
Sensible heat to bring recycled CO2 slip back to reformer conditions. Typical value: 0.88 at Case B
and 1.28 at Case A. Units: MWth. Role: Added to the secondary helium load. Caveat: Calculated.
INL has no equivalent stream because Selexol slip differs.
Variable: Circulator shaft powers ( ˙Ws, ˙Wp)
Shaft work into the secondary and primary helium. Typical value: 3.13 and 3.57 at Case B. Units:
MW. Role: Heat the gas and draw electricity through Eq. B.12. Caveat: No measured circulator
power exists in the library.
Variable: Heat losses ( ˙Qves, ˙Qihx, ˙Qduct)
Loss from the reactor vessel, the IHX and the hot duct. Typical value: 2.66, 0.97 and 0.31 at Case B.
Units: MW. Role: Raise ˙QR in Eq. B.20. Caveat: Each one rests on a single small anchor. The table
prints the screening duct value and Eq. B.14 scales it differently.
Variable: Power conversion eﬀiciency (ηpc)
Electricity per unit of heat in the power branch. Typical value: 0.397, with 0.475 as an alternative.
Units: Dimensionless. Role: Turns electricity demand into reactor heat through Eq. B.19. Caveat:
Interpolated by INL from TEV-981, which is not in the library, so the net or gross status of the value
is unresolved.

Typical value: 0.9005.
Units: Dimensionless.

3
A. Conservation laws
Every balance in this document is one statement applied to a chosen quantity: what accumulates in
a control volume equals what crosses its boundary plus what is made inside. This section sets that
statement down once, reduces it to the forms the engine solves, and fixes the closure tests that every
unit must pass.
29

===== page 31
Equation A.1: General balance
Tier R
d
dt
Z
V
ρψ dV +
I
S
ρψ u · n dS = −
I
S
jψ · n dS +
Z
V
˙sψ dV
(A.1)
Interpretation. The rate of change of ψ held in a fixed volume, plus what the flow carries out, equals
what diffusion brings in plus what is produced inside. Picture a tank with pipes: the level changes
because of the pipes and because of anything made in the tank.
Derivation. Start from a material volume and apply the Reynolds transport theorem to a fixed control
volume, which gives the first two terms. Split the boundary transport into a convective part and a
non-convective flux jψ. Choosing ψ gives each balance used here: ψ = 1 with jψ = 0 and ˙sψ = 0
gives total mass, Eq. A.2. ψ equal to a species mass fraction gives Eq. A.3. ψ = u gives momentum,
Eq. A.11. ψ = e + u2/2 + gz gives energy, Eq. A.7.
Variables. ρ, density, kg m−3; ψ, conserved quantity per unit mass, per kg; u, velocity, m s−1; n,
outward unit normal, 1; jψ, non-convective flux of ψ, per m2 s; ˙sψ, volumetric source of ψ, per m3 s;
V , control volume, m3; S, control surface, m2.
Assumptions. Continuum. Fixed control volume.
Source. [T] Bird, Stewart and Lightfoot [3], ch 3, 11, 19.
Implementation. Theory, no code. Parent of Eq. A.2, Eq. A.3, Eq. A.7 and Eq. A.11.
Validation. ID, test eq_a_01. The reduction in the working above is the identity. Each child equation
carries its own test.
Equation A.2: Total mass
Tier R

X

X

X

X

˙m
(A.2)
Interpretation. Mass in equals mass out once nothing accumulates. It is the first test a stream table
must pass.
Derivation. Set ψ = 1, jψ = 0 and ˙sψ = 0 in Eq. A.1. The volume integral becomes dM/dt. The
surface integral becomes a sum over the openings of ρu · n A, which is the mass flow with its sign.
Variables. M, mass held in the control volume, kg; ˙m, mass flow of a stream, kg s−1.
Assumptions. None beyond Eq. A.1.
Source. [T] As Eq. A.1.
Implementation. plant::flowsheet::check_mass

223,172 lb/h into the Selexol block equals 58,787 plus 164,385 lb/h out, exact to the printed digit.
Equation A.3: Component balance
Tier R
dni
dt =
X
in
Fi −
X
out
Fi +
Z
V
Ri dV
(A.3)
Interpretation. Moles of a species change through flow and through reaction. Reaction is what separates
this from Eq. A.2: a species can appear or vanish while atoms stay fixed.
Derivation. Put ψ equal to the mass fraction of species i in Eq. A.1, with ˙sψ the mass rate of production.
Divide by the molar mass and let the control surface sit where diffusive flux is small against convection,
so the flux term drops at the openings.
Variables. ni, moles of species i held, mol; Fi, molar flow of species i, mol s−1; Ri, volumetric production
rate of i, mol m−3 s−1.

Equation A.4: Source term
Tier R
Ri =
X
j
νijrj,
catalytic: Ri = ρB
X
j
νijηcat
j rj
(A.4)
Interpretation. Each reaction contributes to a species in proportion to its stoichiometric coeﬀicient. In
a packed bed the rate is carried per kilogram of catalyst, so the bed density turns it into a rate per
unit of reactor volume, and the effectiveness factor corrects it for diffusion inside the pellet.
Derivation. One event of reaction j changes species i by νij moles, so summing over reactions gives
the first form with rj per unit volume. For a bed, ρB dV is the catalyst mass in dV , and the observed
rate is ηcat
j rj evaluated at bulk conditions, which gives the second form.
Variables. νij, stoichiometric coeﬀicient of i in reaction j, 1; rj, rate of reaction j, mol m−3 s−1, or
mol kg−1 s−1 per mass of catalyst; ρB, catalyst bed density, kg m−3; ηcat
j , effectiveness factor of
reaction j, 1.
Assumptions. Rates do not depend on the size of the control volume.
Source. [T] Fogler [7]. [P] Xu and Froment Part II [38], Eq 10a, 10b, PDF p. 3.
Implementation. units::xf::Rates::ch4 and units::xf::Rates::co2 sum the reaction rates into
species rates, and units::smr_tube::Tube::eval multiplies them by the bed mass per metre, which
carries ρB and the lumped activity of Eq. D.21
Validation. ID, test eq_a_04. Species rates from Eq. D.19 equal the stoichiometric sum.

Equation A.6: Independent reactions
Tier R
Nr = Ns −rank(a)
(A.6)
Interpretation. Only this many reactions can be set independently. The rest are sums of those, so their
equilibrium constants are products and carry no new information.
Derivation. Allowed changes of composition form the null space of a, and rank plus nullity equals the
number of species. For the reformer the species are CH4, H2O, CO, CO2 and H2 with elements C, H
and O, so Ns = 5, the rank computed here is 3 and Nr = 2. That is why K3 = K1K2 in Eq. D.9. For
aqueous MEA, with MEA, MEAH+, MEACOO−, CO2, HCO−
3 , CO2−
3 , H2O, H3O+ and OH−, charge
joins the rows of a. The rank computed here is 4, because four moieties span the set, so Nr = 5.
Variables. Nr, number of independent reactions, 1; Ns, number of species, 1.
Assumptions. A closed list of species.
Source. [T] As Eq. A.1. Aronu [2], PDF p. 4 prints “four” and lists five reactions. Section M records
this.
Implementation. plant::flowsheet::reaction_rank
Validation. ID, test eq_a_06. Rank by exact arithmetic on the two matrices above, computed here
and repeated by the test named for this card.
Equation A.7: First law

(A.7)
Interpretation. Stored energy changes through heat, shaft work and the energy each stream carries.
Enthalpy appears in place of internal energy because a stream also does flow work at its boundary.
Derivation. Set ψ = e + u2/2 + gz in Eq. A.1. The stress work at the openings is ˙m p/ρ, and adding it
to the internal energy of the stream gives h = e + p/ρ. What remains on the boundary is ˙Q, and the
moving parts inside supply ˙Wsh.
Variables. E, energy held in the control volume, J; ˙Q, heat into the control volume, W;

gravity, m s−2; z, elevation, m.

(A.8)
Interpretation. Each unit in the plant closes on this line. Because hi is on a formation basis, the heat
of reaction needs no separate term: it shows up as the difference between what comes out and what
goes in.
Derivation. Set dE/dt = 0 in Eq. A.7, drop the kinetic and potential terms, and write the mass flows
as molar flows over each species.

Equation A.10: Hess law
Tier R
∆rhj(T) =
X
i
νijhi(T)
(A.10)
Interpretation. The heat of a reaction at any temperature is the enthalpy of its products less that of
its reactants, on a common basis. It shifts with T because products and reactants have different heat
capacities.
Derivation. Apply Eq. A.8 to one event of reaction j at constant temperature, with no shaft work.
Substitute Eq. A.9 to carry the result to any T.
Variables. ∆rhj, enthalpy of reaction j, J mol−1.
Assumptions. As Eq. A.9.
Source. [T] Textbook result. [P] Values in Xu and Froment Part I [37], Table 7, PDF p. 8.
Implementation. Every enthalpy in the engine sits on the formation basis of props::species::h_j_
mol, so the reaction heat enters each balance with no separate term. plant::heatrec::wgs_release_
w evaluates the sum for reaction II

Equation A.11: Momentum in a channel
Tier R
dp
dz = −ρudu
dz −fD
D
ρu2
2
−ρg sin θ
(A.11)
Interpretation. Wall drag usually dominates the pressure loss along a channel. Acceleration of the gas
and lift against gravity make up the rest, and both are small in these loops.
Derivation. Write Eq. A.1 with ψ = u on a slice A dz at steady state. The pressure force per unit volume
is −dp/dz, the convective term is ρu du/dz, gravity gives ρg sin θ, and the wall shear τw πD/A = 4τw/D
is written with the Darcy factor as fDρu2/(2D).
Variables. p, pressure, Pa; fD, Darcy friction factor, 1; D, hydraulic diameter, m; θ, channel inclination,
rad.
Assumptions. 1D, SS.
Source. [T] As Eq. A.1.
Implementation. units::circulator::dp_blasius, on the level channel at constant speed of Eq. C.16
Validation. ID, test eq_a_11. The acceleration and gravity terms vanish for a level channel at constant
speed, leaving the Darcy form used in Eq. C.16.

Interpretation. Gas forcing through catalyst pays a viscous term, which grows with flow, and an inertial

Equation A.14: Gas closure
Tier R
pi = yip,
c =
p
ZRT
(A.14)
Interpretation. A partial pressure is a share of the total. Concentration follows from the gas law, with
Z holding any departure from ideal behaviour.
Derivation. Dalton’s law and the definition of Z. Syngas at reformer conditions is taken as ideal.
Helium uses the correction in Eq. B.1.
Variables. pi, partial pressure of i, Pa; yi, mole fraction of i, 1; c, molar concentration, mol m−3; Z,
compressibility factor, 1; R, gas constant, 8.314462, J mol−1 K−1.
Assumptions. Z = 1 for syngas. Eq. B.1 for helium.
Source. [T] As Eq. A.1.
Implementation. props::mixture::mole_fractions and props::mixture::density_ideal_kg_m3,
with Z = 1 for the process gas. Helium carries its own Z in Eq. B.1
Validation. ID, test eq_a_14. Round trip between pi, yi and c.

Interpretation. These numbers say how far a result sits from obeying the laws above. Limits are 10−8

Worked example: carbon closure at Case B
Use Eq. A.5 for carbon around two control volumes, in kmol h−1. Around the whole plant, 1219.23
enters with the gas. Leaving are 1097.90 captured, 116.90 in the part of the tail gas that is burned
and 4.42 in the hydrogen, which sum to the feed within rounding. Around the reformer, tube carbon
is feed plus recycle: 1219.23 plus 354.82 gives 1574.06. Plant capture is then 1097.90 over 1219.23,
which is 0.9005, the value of ηpl in the Reference Frame. Recycle explains why capture relative to feed
stays close to capture per pass: unconverted carbon gets a second chance at the absorber.
Link to the sources
The closure of INL Case 5 at the Selexol block in Eq. A.2, to the printed digit, shows that the INL
stream tables close as mass balances, so their stream values can serve as anchors. The Hess comparison
in Eq. A.10 shows the Shomate enthalpies sit within 2.3 kJ mol−1 of the values Xu and Froment used.
4
B. HTGR thermal model
This section follows heat from the core to the IHX and asks how much reactor power the plant must
draw. The module is reduced order. It holds no neutronics and no core flow or fuel model: the reactor
appears only as a heat source with an outlet temperature, so nothing here says the core can be built or
licensed at these conditions. Helium is treated with published correlations first, because every balance
after them depends on cp.
Equation B.1: Helium density
Tier E
ρ = 48.14 P
T

1 + 0.4446 P
T 1.2
−1
(B.1)
Interpretation. Helium is almost an ideal gas at reactor conditions. The bracket is the compressibility
factor, which stays within about one percent of unity across the loop.
Fit range and origin. Petersen writes P ·105/(2077.3 ρT) = Z (Eq 3-2, 3-3) and fits Z = 1+0.4446P/T 1.2
(Eq 3-18). Since 105/2077.3 = 48.14, the density follows (Eq 3-19). Petersen states validity from 1 to
100 bar and room temperature to about 1800 K, with a standard deviation of about 0.03% at 1 bar
and 0.3% at 100 bar. The correlation is in bar and K, and the code converts at the function boundary.
Variables. P, helium pressure in bar, in correlations only, bar.
Assumptions. 1 to 100 bar, up to about 1800 K.
Source. [P] Petersen [23], Eq 3-19, printed p. 6, read as an image. [M5]: not in the library.
Implementation. props::helium::density_kg_m3, with Z from props::helium::compressibility
Validation. PUB, test eq_b_01. Computed here at 925 C and 7 MPa: Z = 1.0063 and ρ = 2.795
kg m−3. The OUTRAM reference model gives 1.00643 from its helium equation of state, a project
run that is unpublished, so it serves only as a comparison. Petersen’s own tables: Validation results,

Equation B.2: Helium heat capacities
Tier E
cp = 5195 J kg−1 K−1,
cv = 3117 J kg−1 K−1,
κ = cp/cv = 1.6667
(B.2)
Interpretation. A monatomic gas stores heat only in translation, so its heat capacity barely moves with
temperature or pressure. This is why every helium balance below can use one number.
Fit range and origin. Petersen Eq 4-4 to 4-6. The ideal value is 5R/2M = 5193.2 J kg−1 K−1, computed
here with M = 4.002602 g mol−1, so the printed 5195 sits 1.8 J kg−1 K−1 above it. Petersen gives a
standard deviation of about 0.05% at 273 K and 1 bar, 0.5% at 100 bar, and 0.05% at high temperature
at all pressures. INL stream data imply 5190.
Variables. cp, isobaric heat capacity, J kg−1 K−1; cv, isochoric heat capacity, J kg−1 K−1; κ, heat
capacity ratio, 1.

Interpretation. Conductivity is high for a gas, which makes helium a good coolant, and it climbs with

Equation B.5: Reactor balance
Tier O
˙QR −˙Qves = ˙mpcp (TROT −TRI)
(B.5)
Interpretation. Fission heat that does not leave through the vessel wall goes into the helium. Flow
times heat capacity times temperature rise must match it. This is the only reactor physics in the
model.
Derivation. Apply Eq. A.8 to the core with no shaft work and a constant cp. The heat term is the core
power less the vessel loss. No neutronics and no core flow or fuel model enter, so the equation cannot
say whether a given peak fuel temperature is acceptable.
Variables. TRI, reactor inlet temperature, K.
Assumptions. SS, CP. No core model.
Source. [T] Consequence of Eq. A.8.
Implementation. Not implemented in the engine at this build: the primary flow comes from Eq. B.9,
which leaves out the vessel loss and the circulator rise, so this balance is computed in this document
only. Planned name units::htgr::core_balance.
Validation. PUB, test eq_b_05.
Target: thirteen published designs within 1.5%, HTTR test data
within 6%. Computed here at Case B: left side 175.83 MW, right side 174.93 MW with the flow of
Eq. B.9, a gap of 0.5%. The gap exists because Eq. B.9 ignores the vessel loss and the circulator rise.
A primary flow of 79.6 kg s−1 closes the equation exactly.

Interpretation. Helium is a poor gas to pump. It is light, so raising its pressure a little takes a lot of

Equation B.19: Extraction
Tier O
w = min

E, rw ˙Qgap


˙Qbr = ˙Qgap + w + E −w
ηpc
(B.19)
Interpretation. The reactor must supply the reboiler gap and the electricity from one branch of heat.
Steam bound for the reboiler can first spin a turbine, so part of the electricity comes almost for free.
The rest pays the full conversion penalty.
Derivation. Steam carrying ˙Qgap to the reboiler expands through a back-pressure stage and yields

heat still reaches the reboiler. The remaining electricity E−w needs (E−w)/ηpc of heat from Eq. B.18.
Summing gives ˙Qbr. At Case B, w = 2.22 MW.

Both are well above the 395 C normal limit of the HTTR vessel, TECDOC-1236 [12], Table 3-3.
Equation B.23: INL excess heat
Tier R
˙Qex = ˙mscp (Ts,ret −Ts,ideal)
(B.23)
Interpretation. INL asks how much hotter the helium returns than the process needs. That difference
is heat the reactor could send to power instead.
Derivation. Eq. B.8 between the calculated and the ideal return. TEV-961 text quotes “71 C” and “3
C” at odds with its Table 2, so the table is used, and Section M records the conflict.
Variables. ˙Qex, excess heat for power, W; Ts,ideal, ideal helium return temperature, 461 C, K.
Assumptions. As Eq. B.8.
Source. [P] TEV-961 [36], Table 2 continued, PDF p. 18.
Implementation. vv::gas_bench::tev961 evaluates it with props::helium::duty_w for the five TEV-
961 cases of the validation line
Validation. PUB, test eq_b_23. Computed here: 2.04 MWt at cp = 5190 against the printed 2.0, so
the reactor equivalent is 176.8 plus 2.0, the printed 178.8 MWt. The five ROT cases of TEV-961:
Validation results, 5 rows (T961-2-ROT725C to T961-2-ROT925C): 5 pass. The results mark these
rows as using the same data as the model or as a check of the code, so they are not validation evidence.

===== page 46
Worked example: reactor heat at Case B
Insert the Case B terms into Eq. B.20, in MW: process heat 120.80, slip heat 0.88, IHX loss 0.97,
duct loss 0.31, vessel loss 2.66 and the branch 59.57. Their sum, less the shaft works 3.13 and 3.57,
gives 178.49 MWth. The branch is the term to notice: reboiler gap and electricity together take 59.57
MWth, about half the process heat, because most of that electricity pays the full conversion penalty
at ηpc = 0.397. Against the 210 MWth rating, 31.5 MWth stays idle.
Link to the sources
INL reports 178.8 MWt for its Case 6 with 16.5 MWe imported from the grid, and the circulators and
losses stay outside that figure. This section adds them and the MEA reboiler gap, and it supplies all
electricity from the reactor, so the required reactor at INL’s own scale rises to 260.74 MWth. Ahn
and Lee print a higher process heat of 143.4 MWth on a comparable duty, and the assumptions table
keeps that case beside the central one.
5
C. Intermediate heat exchanger
The IHX passes reactor heat to a clean secondary loop.
Its job here is hard for one reason: the
secondary helium comes back at 466 C, so both ends of the exchanger run on a small temperature
difference. This section takes the exchanger from energy balance to size, then checks the size against
the only IHX that has operated, the HTTR unit.
Equation C.1: Hot side
Tier R
˙Qh = ˙mpcp (Th,in −Th,out)
(C.1)
Interpretation. Heat leaving the primary helium, read from its flow and temperature drop.
Derivation. Eq. A.8 on the hot channels with no shaft work and constant cp.
Variables. ˙Qh, heat lost by the hot stream, W; Th,in, hot stream inlet, K.
Assumptions. SS, CP.
Source. [T] Consequence of Eq. A.8.
Implementation. units::ihx::hot_side

9.48 MW at the 30 MW set and 4.96 MW at the 15 MW set.

Equation C.4: Resistance network
Tier R
1

(C.4)
Interpretation. Heat crosses five barriers in series: two gas films, two fouling layers and the tube wall.
As with resistors in series, the largest one controls.
Derivation. Steady conduction through a cylinder gives a resistance ln(do/di)/(2πkwL) per tube. Con-

tubes, 1.

Implementation. Not implemented in the engine at this build: no film coeﬀicients are published for a
helium IHX at this duty, so the engine sizes the exchanger on the conductance anchor of Eq. C.14.

Interpretation. These are the helical coil correlations behind the HTTR thermal model. They would

Equation C.7: Log-mean temperature difference
Tier R
∆Tlm =
∆T1 −∆T2
ln (∆T1/∆T2)
(C.7)
Interpretation. The driving difference changes along the exchanger, so an average is needed. The right
average is logarithmic because the difference itself decays exponentially with area.
Derivation. Along a counterflow exchanger, d ˙Q = U∆T dA, and each stream changes by d ˙Q/C, so
d(∆T) = −d ˙Q (1/Ch−1/Cc). Substituting the first relation gives d(∆T)/∆T = −U dA (1/Ch−1/Cc).
Integrating from end 1 to end 2 gives ln(∆T2/∆T1) = −UA(1/Ch−1/Cc). The first relation integrated
over the whole duty gives ∆T2 −∆T1 = −˙Q(1/Ch −1/Cc). Dividing removes the bracket and leaves
˙Q = UA ∆Tlm.
Variables. ∆Tlm, log-mean temperature difference, K; ∆T1, terminal difference at end 1, K; ∆T2,
terminal difference at end 2, K; U, overall heat transfer coeﬀicient, W m−2 K−1; A, heat transfer
area, m2; Ch, hot stream capacity rate, W K−1; Cc, cold stream capacity rate, W K−1.
Assumptions. Counterflow, constant U.
Source. [T] Incropera [13].
Implementation. units::ihx::lmtd
47

===== page 49
Validation. ID, PUB, test eq_c_07. Computed here for the HTTR 30 MW set, with end differences of
77 and 135 K: 103.3 K.
Equation C.8: Balanced limit
Tier R
∆Tlm →∆T
as
∆T1 →∆T2 = ∆T
(C.8)
Interpretation. With equal capacity rates the two temperature lines run parallel, so the difference is
the same everywhere and needs no averaging.
Derivation. Put ∆T1 = ∆T(1 + δ) and ∆T2 = ∆T. Then ln(1+ δ) ≈δ for small δ, and Eq. C.7 returns
∆T. The code uses this form when the two ends agree to within rounding, because Eq. C.7 is then
zero over zero.
Variables. ∆T, local temperature difference, K.
Assumptions. Equal capacity rates.
Source. [T] Incropera [13].
Implementation. units::ihx::lmtd
Validation. ID, test eq_c_08. Limit test.
Equation C.9: Effectiveness
Tier R
εHX =
˙Q
Cmin (Th,in −Tc,in)
(C.9)
Interpretation. The share of the thermodynamic maximum that the exchanger achieves. A value of one
would need infinite area.
Derivation. The stream with the smaller capacity rate sees the larger temperature change, and no
stream can change by more than the inlet difference. So Cmin(Th,in −Tc,in) is the largest possible
duty.
Variables. εHX, exchanger effectiveness, 1; Cmin, smaller capacity rate, W K−1.
Assumptions. No loss in the definition.
Source. [T] Incropera [13]. The same definition is INL-11109 [9], Eq 1, PDF p. 12.
Implementation. units::ihx::effectiveness
Validation. PUB, test eq_c_09. Computed here: 0.867 for the HTTR 30 MW set and 0.946 for the
balanced NUS-H2 exchanger, far tighter than the measured value.
Equation C.10: Number of transfer units
Tier R
NTU = UA
Cmin
,
C∗= Cmin
Cmax
(C.10)
Interpretation. NTU says how large the exchanger is against the flow it serves. It measures size with
the flow rate taken out.
Derivation. Definitions. For a balanced exchanger with ˙Q = UA ∆T, it follows that NTU equals the
stream temperature change over the approach.
Variables. NTU, number of transfer units, 1; C∗, capacity ratio, 1; Cmax, larger capacity rate, W K−1.
Assumptions. None.
Source. [T] Incropera [13].

Equation C.11: Counterflow effectiveness
Tier R
εHX =
1 −e−NTU(1−C∗)
1 −C∗e−NTU(1−C∗)
(C.11)
Interpretation. Given size and flows, this predicts the outlet temperatures with no iteration.
The
exchanger model runs on it.
Derivation. Take the cold stream as Cmin. The difference at the end where the hot stream enters is
∆Thot = (Th,in −Tc,in)(1 −εHX), and at the other end it is ∆Tcold = (Th,in −Tc,in)(1 −C∗εHX).
The integration in Eq. C.7 gives ∆Thot/∆Tcold = e−NTU(1−C∗). Solving for εHX gives the statement.
Swapping the roles of the streams gives the same result.
Variables. ˙Q, exchanger duty, W.
Assumptions. Constant U.
Source. [T] Incropera [13].
Implementation. units::ihx::eps_ntu, called by units::ihx::rate
Validation. EXP, test eq_c_11. Computed here for HTTR at 30 MW, with 828 and 248 C inlets, 4.1
and 3.6 kg s−1 and UA = 91 kW K−1: 752 and 385 C, against measured 751 and 383 C, inside 5 K.
At 15 MW with UA = 80 kW K−1: 421 and 237 C against 420 and 235 C. Each UA was derived from
the same data set, so this tests the algebra and the balance, and it is no blind prediction. HTTR-
SR at 905 C has no row and stays a target. The engine rows on the HTTR sets and the INL IHX

4 reported without a limit. The results mark these rows as using the same data as the model or as
a check of the code, so they are not validation evidence. Reported: IHX-INL-Q-CFG1_900C_2MPA

against 60.5 MW.

Equation C.17: Temperature cascade
Tier F
Ts,sup = TROT −∆Tapp −∆Tduct
(C.17)
Interpretation. Each step from core to reformer spends temperature. This line shows how little is left
to spend.
Justification. INL assumes a 25 K approach and no duct drop. At 925 C those give 900 C, and 29 K
above the 871 C reformer exit. A 5 K duct drop leaves 24 K. A 50 K approach leaves 4 K, and with
a 5 K drop the helium falls below the reformer exit, so a fired stage would return.
Variables. ∆Tduct, duct temperature drop, K.
Assumptions. 25 K approach, duct 0 K.
Source. [P] TEV-961 [36], printed p. 10. INL-21537 [21], Table 2, PDF p. 16.
Implementation. units::ihx::cascade
Validation. PUB, test eq_c_17. The figures above are arithmetic on published assumptions. Measured
HTTR hot ends sit at 77 to 82 K.
Equation C.18: Constraints
Tier F
Ts,sup −Tref,out ≥10 K,
Ttube ≤955 ◦C,
∆ptube ≤0.3 MPa
(C.18)
Interpretation. Limits the design must respect. The first is an INL approach rule, and the other two
are HTTR tube limits taken by analogy.
Justification. INL-21537 Table 2 sets a 10 C minimum process approach. Tochio Table 2 gives the
allowable tube temperature of 955 C and differential pressure of 0.3 MPa for Hastelloy XR. They
apply to NUS-H2 only by analogy, because the tube and service life differ.
Variables. Ttube, IHX tube temperature, K, stated in C; ∆ptube, differential pressure across the tube,
Pa.
Assumptions. HTTR limits apply by analogy.
Source. [P] INL-21537 [21], Table 2, PDF p. 16. Tochio and Nakagawa [31], Table 2, PDF p. 5.
Implementation. units::ihx::check_limits
Validation. ID, test eq_c_18. A test sets each limit and reads the flag. Reported: a 50 K approach
leaves a 4 K pinch and fails the first limit.

===== page 53
Equation C.19: Pressure sign
Tier F
ps ≥pp + 0.1 MPa
(HTTR)
(C.19)
Interpretation. HTTR keeps the secondary helium above the primary, so a tube leak pushes clean gas
into the reactor circuit and contains radioactivity. The project leaves its own sign to the safety case.
Justification. IAEA-TECDOC-1382 printed p. 8 states the HTTR rule and its reason.
INL-11109
makes the same argument for a 7.1 MPa loop on PDF p. 16. No safety case exists for NUS-H2, so the
balance holds both loops at 7.0 MPa and marks the sign as open.
Variables. ps, secondary helium pressure, Pa; pp, primary helium pressure, Pa.
Assumptions. An open item.
Source. [P] IAEA-TECDOC-1382 [11], printed p. 8, PDF p. 14.
Implementation. Not implemented in the engine at this build: the balance holds both loops at 7.0 MPa
and checks no pressure sign. Planned as a further flag of units::ihx::check_limits.
Validation. NONE, test eq_c_19. No benchmark. The item stays open for the safety case.
Worked example: sizing the IHX at Case B
Take the balanced design with 120.80 MW and a 25 K approach at both ends. Eq. C.8 gives ∆Tlm = 25
K, so Eq. C.13 gives UA =4.83 MW K−1. Each stream changes by 434 K, so NTU = 17.4 by Eq. C.10
and εHX = 0.946 by Eq. C.12. At the HTTR scale of 400 W m−2 K−1, the surface is 12,080 m2. The
measured HTTR conductance is 85 kW K−1, so Case B needs 57 HTTR units in conductance. Four
modules bring that to 14 per module, and the unbalanced option at a 600 C primary outlet brings
the total to 22 at the cost of a hotter reactor inlet.
Link to the sources
Tochio and Nakagawa report a stable UA of about 85 kW K−1 over a 50-day high-temperature run,
with no sign of degradation. That is the strongest evidence for this class of machine. It also marks
the distance to this design: the balanced approach of 25 K sits a factor of three below the 77 to 82
K measured hot ends, and INL adopted 25 K as an assumption. The IHX therefore stays a labelled
risk, and the 50 K sensitivity is carried through the cascade of Eq. C.17.
6
D. Steam methane reformer
The reformer is where nuclear heat becomes chemical energy. Two descriptions sit side by side here.
The plant ledger uses equilibrium at the INL exit condition, Eq. D.11 at 871 C, with the INL heat
basis. The Xu and Froment tube model then tests that assumption. It runs outside its fitted range,
so its cards carry tier O, and no tube-model result enters the headline ledger.
Equation D.1: Reaction I
Tier R
CH4 + H2O −−⇀
↽−−CO + 3 H2,
∆rh◦
298 = +206.1 kJ mol−1
(D.1)
Interpretation. Methane and steam make synthesis gas. The reaction takes in a large amount of heat
and raises the number of moles, so it wants high temperature and low pressure.
Derivation. Stoichiometry from Xu and Froment Part I Table 2. The heat comes from Table 7. Table
2 lists −∆H and Table 7 lists +∆H, so the sign must be read with care. Here it is positive for an
endothermic reaction.
Variables. ∆rh◦
298, standard enthalpy of reaction at 298 K, J mol−1.
52

===== page 54
Assumptions. None.
Source. [P] Xu and Froment Part I [37], Table 2, PDF p. 3, and Table 7, PDF p. 8.
Implementation. props::equilibrium::Basis::amounts and units::smr_tube::Tube::flows carry
it as the extent of reaction I
Validation. ID, test eq_d_01. Element balance by Eq. A.5.
Equation D.2: Reaction II, shift
Tier R

↽−−CO2 + H2,
∆rh◦
298 = −41.15 kJ mol−1
(D.2)
Interpretation. Carbon monoxide takes oxygen from steam and frees more hydrogen. The heat released
is small, so the reaction moves mainly with temperature through its equilibrium constant.
Derivation. As Eq. D.1. Section E applies this reaction in the shift beds.

298, standard enthalpy of reaction at 298 K, J mol−1.

Equation D.3: Reaction III
Tier R
CH4 + 2 H2O −−⇀
↽−−CO2 + 4 H2,
∆rh◦
298 = +164.9 kJ mol−1
(D.3)
Interpretation. The direct route to CO2. It is the sum of the first two reactions, so it adds no thermo-
dynamic freedom, though Xu and Froment give it its own rate.
Derivation. Add Eq. D.1 and Eq. D.2. The heats add to 164.95, which rounds to the printed value.
Variables. ∆rh◦
298, standard enthalpy of reaction at 298 K, J mol−1.
Assumptions. None.
Source. [P] Xu and Froment Part I [37], Table 2, PDF p. 3, and Table 7, PDF p. 8.
Implementation. units::xf::Kinetics::rates, as the third rate with K3 of Eq. D.9. The equilibrium
solve needs only the two independent extents of Eq. A.6
Validation. ID, test eq_d_03. Sum of Eq. D.1 and Eq. D.2.
Equation D.4: Heavier gas
Tier O
CnHm + n H2O →n CO +

n + m
2

H2
(D.4)
Interpretation. Ethane and heavier gases reform much faster than methane, so they are taken to
completion at the tube inlet and only methane is tracked after that.
Derivation. EVA measurements quoted in TECDOC-1236 show 30 to 40% of the ethane gone after 1
m of catalyst and all of it by 4 m. A heavier fraction therefore reaches the reaction zone only briefly.
Natural gas in TEV-953 holds 93.571% methane, 3.749% ethane, 0.920% propane, 0.260% butane,
0.040% pentane and 0.010% hexane by mole, with 0.250% CO2. Counting carbon gives 1.05379 mol
of carbon per mole of gas.
Variables. n, carbon atoms in the hydrocarbon, 1; m, hydrogen atoms in the hydrocarbon, 1.

Equation D.5: Feed ratio
Tier F
S/C = FH2O
FC
= 3.0
(D.5)
Interpretation. Excess steam keeps carbon off the catalyst and pushes conversion up. Each extra unit

Justification. INL sets the steam-to-carbon ratio at 3.0 to avoid carbon formation. The Case 6 sheets

Equation D.6: Mole table from extents
Tier R
nCH4 = 1 −x1,
nCO = x1 −x2,
nCO2 = ξCO2 + x2,
nH2 = ξH2 + 3x1 + x2,
nH2O = SC −x1 −x2,
ntot = Σ0 + 2x1
(D.6)
Interpretation. Two numbers fix the whole composition once the feed is known: how far reaction I has
gone and how far the shift has gone. Everything else follows from counting atoms.
Derivation. Take one mole of methane fed with SC moles of steam, ξCO2 of CO2 and ξH2 of H2.
Reaction I at extent x1 removes x1 of CH4 and H2O and adds x1 of CO and 3x1 of H2. Reaction II
at extent x2 moves x2 from CO and H2O to CO2 and H2. Reaction III needs no separate extent by
Eq. A.6. Reaction I adds two moles per event and reaction II adds none, so the total is Σ0 + 2x1 with
Σ0 = 1 + SC + ξCO2 + ξH2 plus any inert.
Variables. ni, moles of i per mole of CH4 fed, 1; x1, extent of reaction I, which is total methane
conversion, 1; x2, extent of reaction II, 1; ξCO2, CO2 fed per mole of CH4, 1; ξH2, H2 fed per mole of
CH4, 1; SC, steam fed per mole of CH4, 1; Σ0, total moles fed per mole of CH4, 1.
Assumptions. x1 and x2 are the extents of reactions I and II.
Source. [T] Textbook result. The same split of conversions is in Xu and Froment Part II [38], PDF
p. 3 and Notation.
Implementation. props::equilibrium::Basis::amounts and props::equilibrium::Basis::total,
on the feed flows in mol s−1, which carry this table scaled by the CH4 fed
Validation. ID, test eq_d_06. Element balance by Eq. A.5.

Equation D.11: Equilibrium
Tier R
Q1 (x1, x2, p) = K1(T),
Q2 (x1, x2) = K2(T)
at the exit T, p
(D.11)
Interpretation. Two equations and two unknowns. Solving them gives the composition the catalyst
would reach given enough time, which is the upper limit of conversion at the exit condition.
Derivation. Substitute Eq. D.6 into Eq. D.10 with pi = pyi. In logarithms, 3 ln nH2 +ln nCO −ln nCH4 −
ln nH2O + 2 ln(p/ntot) = ln K1 and ln nH2 + ln nCO2 −ln nCO −ln nH2O = ln K2. Solve by damped
Newton on (x1, x2), keeping each mole number positive.
Variables. ntot, total moles per mole of CH4 fed, 1.
Assumptions. Isobaric, IG.
Source. [T] Textbook result. INL uses Gibbs minimisation, TEV-953 [35], PDF p. 18.

Equation D.12: Thermodynamic consistency
Tier R
d ln K
d(1/T) = −∆rh
R
(D.12)
Interpretation. The slope of ln K against 1/T is the heat of reaction. An exponential fit therefore
carries an implied heat, which can be checked against Hess.
Derivation. From ln K = −∆rg/RT and the Gibbs-Helmholtz relation. A pure exponential in 1/T has
a constant slope, so the fit implies a constant ∆rh across its range.

from Gibbs energies, so they meet this relation by construction

Interpretation. Each adsorbed species occupies part of the nickel surface. Adsorption grows weaker as

has. It is squared in each rate.

term is a ratio to hydrogen because it describes the oxygen coverage set by the dissociation of steam.

Equation D.36: Hot end
Tier F
∆Thot = Ts,sup −Tref,out = 29 K
(D.36)
65

===== page 67
Interpretation. Only 29 K separates the helium from the gas leaving the catalyst. No tube has run at
this approach, and conversion depends on it.
Justification. INL’s 871 C exit and 900 C supply give this number, from a Gibbs result with no tube
model. The JAERI mock-up measured 102 to 125 K against a design of 100 K. The project keeps 871
C and carries sensitivities at 825 and 800 C, so the risk stays visible.
Variables. ∆Thot, reformer hot-end approach, K.
Assumptions. INL Gibbs result, no tube model.
Source. [P] TEV-961 [36], PDF p. 14 and p. 18. Measured values from JAERI [28], Table 3.3, PDF
p. 28.
Implementation. units::ihx::check_limits returns this difference, with the exit of plant::
flowsheet::T_REFORMER_OUT_C
Validation. PUB, test eq_d_36. Computed here: equilibrium conversion of 80.3, 70.2 and 64.3% at
871, 825 and 800 C. Those lower values test the assumption, and this document draws no forecast of
plant conversion from them. The flowsheet runs at the three exits give L5 at 25 y of S$189.7, 204.8
and 217.0/t. On the assumed tube of Eq. D.35, no count up to 5000 tubes reaches 871 C, so that exit
rests on the INL Gibbs result alone.
Worked example: equilibrium at the reformer exit
Take one mole of pure methane at 871 C (1144.15 K), 30.3 bar and S/C = 3. Eq. D.7 and Eq. D.8 give
K1 = 784.1 bar2 and K2 = 0.8268. Solving Eq. D.11 gives a methane conversion x1 of 80.3%, with
x2 = 0.295 and ntot = 5.606. The wet composition is H2 48.2, H2O 33.9, CO 9.1, CO2 5.3 and CH4
3.5 mol%, close to the INL raw syngas printed in TEV-953. Hydrogen before shift is 3x1 + x2 = 2.70
mol per mole of methane. Dropping the exit to 825 C takes conversion to 70.2%, and 800 C takes it
to 64.3%, which is why the hot-end approach in Eq. D.36 is the first risk to read in this section.
Link to the sources
Xu and Froment built their rates on 3 to 15 bar and 773 to 848 K, with a catalyst crushed to remove
diffusion. Applying them at 1144 K and 30 bar is an extrapolation, and the JAERI mock-up shows
that exit composition cannot test it, since the rig runs near equilibrium at its hot end. The tube
model therefore checks the heat path, and the plant result stands on the equilibrium of Eq. D.11,
which reproduces TECDOC-1236 and INL within the margins stated on that card. Zhang et al. Table
1 is the source of K(T) because the NIST data agree with it inside 5.3% across the range.
7
E. Water-gas shift
Shift turns the carbon monoxide left by the reformer into CO2 and more hydrogen. INL runs two
adiabatic beds in series in all its reforming cases, a high-temperature bed followed by a low-temperature
bed, and the plant keeps that layout. The Case 6 sheets are missing, so operating points come from
TEV-961 Case 5 as a proxy. Both beds are written as an approach to equilibrium. The Chinchen rate
data for iron-chromium catalyst size the first bed as a check, and the library holds no rate law for the
copper-zinc catalyst that a bed at this temperature would use.
In this section kHTS replaces the plain k of the source, because k is thermal conductivity elsewhere,
and XCO replaces Chinchen’s C. Numbers tagged [C] are hand arithmetic on printed source values,
shown so that a reader can redo them. Implementation lines name the engine items that carry the
card’s doc tag, or say that the engine does not implement the card. The engine test run of this version
is stated in the Reference Frame. A benchmark that a card states without a results outcome stays a
target.
66

===== page 68
Equation E.1: CO conversion
Tier R
XCO = FCO,in −FCO,out
FCO,in
(E.1)
Interpretation. The share of incoming carbon monoxide that a bed consumes. It can be read across
one bed or across the whole train.
Derivation. This is a definition. Shift keeps the total moles constant, so on wet mole fractions it reduces
to XCO = 1 −yCO,out/yCO,in, which is the form that a printed stream table allows.
Variables. XCO, CO conversion, 1; FCO,in, CO molar flow into the bed, mol s−1; FCO,out, CO molar
flow out of the bed, mol s−1.
Assumptions. None.
Source. [T] Definition. Stream values from IEAGHG 2017-02 [1], PDF p. 44.
Implementation. Not implemented in the engine at this build: the shift beds return the extent, and
the conversion is computed in this document. Planned name units::wgs::conversion.
Validation. PUB, test eq_e_01. Target: 68% across the IEAGHG high-temperature bed. Streams 5
and 6 print yCO falling from 0.1156 to 0.0366 at constant molar flow, which gives 68.3% [C].
Equation E.2: Species from one extent
Tier R
Fi = Fi,in + νiξ,
νCO = νH2O = −1,
νCO2 = νH2 = +1
(E.2)
Interpretation. One number fixes the whole outlet composition. Each mole of shift removes a mole of
CO and of steam and adds a mole of CO2 and of hydrogen.
Derivation. Eq. A.3 at steady state, integrated over the bed with Eq. D.2 as the only reaction. Methane
and nitrogen carry νi = 0, so they pass through unchanged. The stoichiometric coeﬀicients sum to
zero, so the total molar flow stays constant across a bed.
Variables. ξ, extent of shift across a bed, mol s−1.
Assumptions. SS. One reaction, with no methanation.
Source. [T] Consequence of Eq. A.3 and Eq. D.2.
Implementation. units::wgs::shifted
Validation. ID, test eq_e_02. Element balance by Eq. A.5 and constant total moles.
Equation E.3: Equilibrium limit
Tier R
Q2 (Tout) ≤K2 (Tout)
(E.3)
Interpretation. A bed can approach equilibrium at its exit temperature and can never pass it. Shift
releases heat, so an adiabatic bed lowers its own limit as it warms.
Derivation. By the second law the reaction runs forward only while the quotient of Eq. D.10 stays
below K2. Eq. D.8 falls as temperature rises, while the quotient rises with ξ, so the two curves cross
at one extent for a given feed.
Variables. Tout, gas temperature at the bed exit, K.
Assumptions. IG.
Source. [T] Second law. K2 from Eq. D.8, Zhang et al. [41], Table 1, PDF p. 5.
Implementation. units::wgs::extent_at
Validation. ID, test eq_e_03. The solver never returns Q2 above K2 at the exit.

Equation E.4: Approach to equilibrium
Tier O
Q2 (Tout) = K2 (Tout + ∆Teq) ,
∆Teq = 10 K
(E.4)
Interpretation. The exit gas sits where the equilibrium curve runs 10 K hotter than the gas itself. One
number stands in for finite catalyst activity on both beds.
Derivation. Three printed outlets fix the value. Solving K2(T) = Q2 with Eq. D.8 gives the equilibrium
temperature of each stream [C]. Case 5 high-temperature bed: raw gas from sheet A-50 and 3.01 mol%
dry CO from A-53 give Q2 = 10.56 against K2 = 11.43 at 764 F, a ratio of 0.92 and a gap of 8.5 K.
Case 5 low-temperature bed: 0.33 mol% dry CO gives Q2 = 122.8 against K2 = 151.3 at 415 F, a
ratio of 0.81 and a gap of 11.4 K. IEAGHG high-temperature bed: Q2 = 9.78 against K2 = 10.87 at
412 C, a ratio of 0.90 and a gap of 11.5 K. The gaps run from 8.5 to 11.5 K, so 10 K is a round central
value carried from two independent flowsheets.
Variables. ∆Teq, equilibrium approach temperature, K.
Assumptions. Same approach on both beds.
Source. [C] Calculated here from TEV-961 [36], A-50 and A-53, PDF p. 74 and p. 77, and IEAGHG
2017-02 [1], PDF p. 44.
Implementation. units::wgs::lts applies the approach on the second bed. units::wgs::hts in-
tegrates Eq. E.7 on the first bed, and can size it to stop at the IEAGHG ratio of units::wgs::
ieaghg_hts_q_over_k, in which case the third anchor sets that exit
Validation. PUB, test eq_e_04.
Q2/K2 of 0.92 for the high-temperature bed, 0.81 for the low-

Equation E.7: High-temperature rate
Tier E
dpCO
dtr
= −kHTS (pCO −pCO,eq)
(E.7)
Interpretation. The rate is proportional to how far CO sits above its equilibrium level. It closes to zero
at equilibrium, as it must.
Fit range and origin. Fit form from semi-technical runs on ICI catalyst 15, in two pellet sizes, with 350
ml of catalyst at 310 to 450 C and 16 to 31 bar. The feed was 1.75 m3/h of dry gas at 20 C and 1 bar,
at a steam ratio of 1.0, and the gas was nominally 80% H2, 10% CO and 10% CO2. Chinchen prints
the equation with no minus sign, so k there is a rate of disappearance. The sign is written out here.
Variables. kHTS, pseudo-first-order shift constant, empty bed basis, s−1; pCO,eq, CO partial pressure
at equilibrium, Pa; tr, contact time on the empty bed, s.
Assumptions. Pseudo first order.
Source. [P] Chinchen et al. [4], PDF p. 6, printed p. 74. Feed from PDF p. 5.
Implementation. units::wgs::hts_extent, which integrates the rate along the bed volume
Validation. EXP, test eq_e_07. Chinchen Table 1, PDF p. 6. Table 1 is calibration data, so CH-1 tests
the fit only. Validation results, 4 rows (CH-1 to W-2): 2 pass, 2 not run. The results mark 1 of these
rows as using the same data as the model, and 2 as independent. CH-1 is a calibration row, so it tests
the fit only.
Equation E.8: Integrated rate
Tier E
kHTS = Sv ln

1
1 −XCO/XCO,e

(E.8)
Interpretation. This turns a measured conversion into a rate constant. A conversion close to equilibrium
needs a long contact time, and the logarithm shows how fast that demand grows.
Fit range and origin. Separate Eq. E.7 and integrate from tr = 0 to tr = 1/Sv at constant kHTS and
pCO,eq, which gives ln[(pCO,0 −pCO,eq)/(pCO −pCO,eq)] = kHTS/Sv. Conversion is linear in pCO, so
each difference becomes a difference in conversion, and the bracket follows. Chinchen defines both k
and Sv on empty bed volume at reaction conditions.
Variables. Sv, space velocity at reaction conditions, empty bed, s−1; XCO,e, CO conversion at equilib-
rium, 1.
Assumptions. Plug flow, isothermal. Steam ratio 1.0 in the source against S/DG = 0.50 here, with no
correction.
Source. [P] Chinchen et al. [4], PDF p. 6 for the equation and PDF p. 7 for the definitions.

(E.9)
Interpretation. The measured rate constant in s−1. It rises between thirtyfold and sixtyfold across the
range, and the larger pellet falls behind the smaller one above about 350 C, where the gas can no

Fit range and origin. Table 1 read from the page image. S is the 5.4 by 3.6 mm pellet, type 15-5,

Interpretation. A rough size for the first bed. It answers one question only: whether the assumed

Derivation. Put Sv = ˙V /Vcat in Eq. E.8. At Case 5 the gas flow is 6.0 m3 s−1 at the inlet and 7.0 m3 s−1

Equation E.13: Steam to dry gas ratio
Tier F
S/DG =
FH2O
P
dry Fi
(E.13)
Interpretation. The steam carried by each mole of dry gas. It pushes shift forward and protects the
catalyst, and it falls from one bed to the next because shift consumes steam.
Justification. INL prints 0.50 with a minimum of 0.4 at the high-temperature inlet, and 0.37 with a
minimum of 0.3 at the low-temperature inlet. TEV-953 says the 0.30 limit keeps good catalyst activity
while inhibiting Fischer-Tropsch reactions.
Variables. S/DG, steam to dry gas mole ratio, 1.

Equation E.14: Temperature window
Tier F
THTS,in ≥600 F,
TLTS,in ≥max (375 F, Tdp + 27 F)
(E.14)
Interpretation. Each bed needs its inlet warm enough to stay active, and the second bed must also stay
clear of liquid water.
Justification. In SI the limits are 588.7 K and 463.7 K, with a dew margin of 15 K. Case 5 runs 635 F
and 375 F against a printed dew point of 331 F, a margin of 44 F. Eq. F.3 gives the dew point.
Variables. THTS,in, high-temperature bed inlet, K; TLTS,in, low-temperature bed inlet, K; Tdp, dew
point, K.
Assumptions. INL limits carried unchanged.
Source. [P] TEV-961 [36], A-53, PDF p. 77, which prints the limits. Operating values also in TEV-953
[35], PDF p. 18 and p. 19.
Implementation. Not implemented in the engine at this build: a bed inlet is refused only when it is
two-phase or short of CO or steam. Planned name units::wgs::check_window.
Validation. ID, test eq_e_14. The window check trips on inlets set just outside each limit.
Equation E.15: Low-temperature bed
Tier O
Q2 (TLTS,out) = K2 (TLTS,out + ∆Teq) ,
with Eq. E.5 across the bed
(E.15)
Interpretation. The second bed has no kinetics at all. Its exit is set by the equilibrium approach and
the energy balance.
Derivation. Solve Eq. E.2, Eq. E.4 and Eq. E.5 together for ξ and TLTS,out. The library holds no
copper-zinc rate law, so a rate form cannot be written here. TECDOC-1236 states only that copper-
zinc catalyst allows 200 to 240 C.
Variables. TLTS,out, low-temperature bed exit, K.
Assumptions. Equilibrium approach, anchored on INL operating points.

(E.16)
Interpretation. A published rate law for iron-chromium catalyst on biomass gas.
It is printed for
completeness and takes no part in the plant.
72

===== page 74
Fit range and origin. Kraussler Eq 2 with k0 = 117.8 mol g−1 Pa−1.71 s−1 from Table 1. Table 3 raises
Ea,K from 101.9 to 126.6 kJ mol−1, changing that parameter alone on one operating point. The rig
ran at ambient pressure on real gas holding H2S and tar. The orders sum to 1.71, so taking the law
to 28 bar would multiply the rate by about 300, and Kraussler attributes the CO order of 1.77 to its
low pressure, against about 1.0 elsewhere. That is why the law does not extrapolate to this plant.
Kraussler’s KMAL and Kg are Q2 and K2 here.
Variables. r,
shift rate per unit mass of catalyst,
mol g−1 s−1;

pre-exponential factor,
mol g−1 Pa−1.71 s−1; Ea,K, activation energy of this law, J mol−1.
Assumptions. Ambient pressure, biomass gas.
Source. [P] Kraussler et al. [16], Eq 2 and Table 1, PDF p. 2, and Table 3, PDF p. 8.
Implementation. none. The equation is listed only.
Validation. NONE, test eq_e_16. No plant benchmark. The validation matrix keeps one ambient
comparison, KR-1, on Kraussler’s own rig as a weak test of the code.
Validation results, 4 rows
(KR-1-OP1 to KR-1-OP4): 4 not run.
Worked example: Case 5 shift, carried to Case B
Take 100 mol of Case 5 raw syngas from sheet A-50: H2 48.8, CO 8.7, CO2 4.8, H2O 33.4, CH4 3.6
and N2 0.7. The dry gas is 66.6 mol, so S/DG = 0.50 by Eq. E.13.
First bed. A-53 prints 3.01 mol% dry CO at the exit. Each mole of shift removes one mole of CO
and adds one mole of dry gas, so (8.7 −ξ)/(66.6 + ξ) = 0.0301 and ξ = 6.50 mol. By Eq. E.2 the gas
becomes H2 55.30, CO 2.20, CO2 11.30 and H2O 26.90, with XCO = 74.7% by Eq. E.1. The quotient
of Eq. D.10 is 10.56, close to the K2 of 11.43 at 764 F.
Second bed. A-53 prints 0.33 mol% dry CO, so ξ = 1.95 mol. The gas becomes H2 57.25, CO 0.25,
CO2 13.25 and H2O 24.95, with 88.7% conversion across this bed and 97.2% across both. On a dry
basis the gas holds 17.66 mol% CO2, 76.3 mol% H2, 4.80 mol% CH4 and 0.33 mol% CO, which matches
the 17.7 mol% printed at the Selexol inlet. The second bed adds 3.5% more hydrogen and 17% more
CO2 for capture, measured on the first bed outlet.
Heat. The mean molar mass is 12.32 g mol−1, so 349,724 lb h−1 is 3,576 mol s−1, which sits within
0.3% of the 3,568 mol s−1 from the printed 258 MMSCFD. Eq. E.6 then gives 232.4 mol s−1 at 38.5
kJ mol−1 on the first bed and 69.8 mol s−1 at 39.8 kJ mol−1 on the second, or 8.9 and 2.8 MW.
Scaling by 1,927/1,970 for captured CO2 and by s = 0.6833 for Case B gives 6.0 and 1.9 MW, a total
of 7.8 MW. All of this is hand arithmetic on the Case 5 proxy.
Link to the sources
INL and IEAGHG reach almost the same outlet from different feeds, and the printed streams of both
fall within 2 K of a 10 K approach to equilibrium. That agreement is the evidence behind Eq. E.4.
Chinchen supports the first bed at the right pressure and a steam ratio twice the plant value. No
library source supports the rate of the second bed, so Eq. E.15 stands on operating points alone, which
is recorded as [M4].
8
F. Syngas cooling and separation
After the second shift bed the gas falls from 213 C to 40 C and most of its water condenses. The INL
sheets give the full train for Case 5, which stands in for the missing Case 6. Two results matter most
for the plant balance: the heat that can reach the reboiler and the dry feed to the absorber. The
condensate is tracked alongside them.
Inside this section Tin and Tout replace plain numbered temperatures, and the flash uses βV with Ff,
Vf and Lf, because V and L already name a control volume and a tube length. Numbers tagged [C]
73

===== page 75
are hand arithmetic on printed values. Implementation lines name the engine items that carry the
card’s doc tag, or say that the engine does not implement the card. The engine test run of this version
is stated in the Reference Frame. A benchmark that a card states without a results outcome stays a
target. The Case 5 duties are printed in MMBtu h−1, and 1 MMBtu h−1 is 0.29307 MW.

Equation F.3: Dew point
Tier R
yH2O p = psat (Tdp)
(F.3)
Interpretation. Water starts to condense when its partial pressure reaches the saturation pressure.
Above that temperature Eq. F.1 holds. Below it the latent heat dominates.
Derivation. Raoult’s law for a nearly pure liquid water phase, with γ = 1 and xw = 1. The ideal gas
sets the partial pressure.
Variables. yH2O, water mole fraction in the gas, 1; psat, water saturation pressure, Pa.

===== page 76
Assumptions. Raoult, γ = 1.
Source. [T] Textbook result.
Implementation. The dew test inside units::flash::split, at the set temperature. The engine solves
for no Tdp
Validation. PUB, test eq_f_03. Target: 331 F printed on A-53, which is 166 C. Hand estimate at the
second bed inlet: yH2O = 0.269 at 413 psi gives 7.66 bar of water, which saturates near 169 C [C],
about 3 K above the printed value.
Equation F.4: Saturation pressure
Tier E
psat (T) = IAPWS-IF97 saturation line
(F.4)
Interpretation. Water properties come from the international steam tables through a maintained library.
Fit range and origin. The library holds no water saturation table and no Antoine coeﬀicients. The
screening Antoine fit is carried no further, because its constants are rated from 99 to 374 C and it
gives 6.10 kPa at 35 C, against 5.63 kPa on the project input sheet. The maintained source is IF97
through seuif97, under an MIT licence.
Variables. psat(T), saturation pressure at T, Pa.
Assumptions. Screening Antoine not carried.
Source. [M9]: no water saturation data in the library, IF97 used in its place.
Implementation. props::steam::psat_pa
Validation. PUB, test eq_f_04. The IF97 verification table. Validation results, 1 row (PROPS-IF97-
VECTORS): 1 pass. The results mark these rows as using the same data as the model or as a check
of the code, so they are not validation evidence.

CO2 row: Validation results, 1 row (T961-8-CO2): 1 pass.
Worked example: heat and water at Case B
Case 5 sets the shape of the train, and Case B follows by the factor 1,927/1,970 for captured CO2 and
the factor s = 0.6833 on product, which together give 0.668.
Reboiler heat. WGS-EX4 passes the 130 C limit of the plant with 11.5 MW above it by the reading
used in Eq. F.11. Scaling gives 11.25 MW at Case A and 7.69 MW at Case B. That heat is one layer
78

===== page 80
of the plant steam credit of 37.9 MW against a reboiler of 51.0 MW, which leaves the gap of 13.1 MW
that the reactor must carry. The temperature limit matters more than the duty: all of WGS-EX3
runs above 130 C and INL already spends it on LP steam, while most of WGS-EX4 runs too cold to
be useful.
Water. The drum condenses 57.4 t h−1 at Case 5, which scales to 38.4 t h−1 at Case B. Of this,
3.9 t h−1 returns to the reformer and 34.4 t h−1 goes to treatment by Eq. F.10. Dissolved CO2 by
Eq. F.12 scales to about 190 kg h−1. The dry composition of Eq. F.13 stays the same, so the absorber
sees 4.74 bar of CO2 at either scale.
Link to the sources
Sheets A-55 and A-56 are the only complete cooling train in the library. IEAGHG shows the same
layout down to the condensate separator, and its flowsheet with one shift bed leaves about 4.6 mol%
CO in the same stream, which is why the second shift bed matters for this project. Water saturation
and gas solubility rest on outside data, recorded as [M9] and [M8].
9
G. MEA thermodynamics
The solvent model sets how much CO2 each kilogram of solvent can hold and how hard it is to release.
The selected model is the extended UNIQUAC of Aronu et al. [2], Chem. Eng. Sci. 66 (2011) 6393,
with corrigendum 78 (2012) 246, cited below as Aronu. It carries tier R inside 15 to 60 mass% MEA
and 40 to 120 C. The e-NRTL model of Zhang et al. [40], Fluid Phase Equilib. 311 (2011) 67, is left
out because it needs Aspen Databank values that no local source prints, so its figures stay as read-offs
for comparison.
Aronu prints the parameters and the top level of the model, and leaves the activity expressions to
Thomsen (1997) and Thomsen and Rasmussen (1999), which the library lacks. The expressions in
Eq. G.14, Eq. G.15 and Eq. G.17 therefore come from general knowledge of that framework, recorded
as [M7].
Their support is indirect: with them, a prototype reproduces Aronu’s published model
column at 104 points. That prototype is a reading aid that ran on the printed tables. The Rust port
in the mea crate has its own tests, and the run recorded for this version is stated in the Reference
Frame. The benchmark rows of the solvent model are in the validation results file. A benchmark that
a card states without a results outcome stays a target.
Symbols: Kx,j is the mole fraction equilibrium constant, because Kj names adsorption in Section D.
Hx is the Henry constant on a mole fraction basis, in Pa. Zi is ion charge, zc the coordination number,
and ADH, bDH and σDH the Debye-Hückel terms. Implementation lines name the engine items that
carry the card’s doc tag, or say that the engine does not implement the card.

Tier R
2 H2O −−⇀

Interpretation. Dissolved CO2 reacts on into bicarbonate and carbamate while MEA picks up protons.
That chemistry is where the capacity of the solvent comes from.
Derivation. Aronu Eqs 4 to 8, with RNH2 written as MEA. The carbamate is explicit, so nine species
follow: H2O, MEA, CO2, H3O+, MEAH+, OH−, HCO−
3 , CO2−
3
and MEACOO−. Aronu’s text says

Interpretation. Each reaction has its own temperature curve. The strong fall of the MEA and carbamate
constants with cooling is what makes the solvent absorb at 40 C and release at 120 C.
Fit range and origin. Aronu Eq 16 and Table 2, read from the page image, because the text layer drops
minus signs. Aronu states that the constants are on a mole fraction basis, so they are dimensionless,
while Hx carries pascal. KMEA is rated for 0 to 50 C and Hx for 0 to 100 C, yet both run to 120 C
here. That is an extrapolation inside the Aronu regression itself.

Chem. Eng. Sci. 43 (1988) 573, Table 10, PDF p. 12. Hand check at 298.15 K: ln Kx,MEA = −25.882,
so pKx = 11.24, and removing log10(1/Mw) = 1.74 gives 9.50 [C].

Equation G.7: Hidden convention on the MEA constant
Tier O
ln Kx,MEA,eff = ln Kx,MEA + ln γ∞
MEA
(G.7)
Interpretation. Aronu’s MEA constant is an aqueous pKa, while the paper’s stated reference for MEA
is the pure liquid. This one line reconciles the two, and without it the model misses the published
column by about 60%.
Derivation. Table 2 takes KMEA from Bates and Pinching, where MEA is referred to infinite dilution in
water, so its activity there is γ∗
MEAxMEA. On the stated pure-liquid basis the activity is γMEAxMEA.
Then Eq. G.6 gives the factor γ∞
MEA on K. Aronu does not state this. A project calculation found it
from a ratio that was independent of loading and depended on temperature. Two independent values
support it: ln γ∞
MEA at 313.15 K is −1.279 from Aronu’s UNIQUAC and −1.320 from the NRTL of
Zhang et al. Table 8, and Cownden and Lucquiaud [5], Carbon Capture Sci. Technol. 19 (2026) 100625,
Table 1, regressed a nearby constant on a solvent reference. KMEACOO stays as printed.
Variables. Kx,MEA,eff, MEA constant on the pure liquid basis, 1.
Assumptions. Not stated by Aronu.
Source. [C] Calculated here. Support from Cownden and Lucquiaud Table 1 and Zhang et al. Table
8.
Implementation. mea::aronu::Thermo::ln_k_mea_effective
Validation. PUB, test eq_g_07. Prototype figures at 30 mass%, 17 model column points: AARD 58.8%
without this line and 0.11% with it. The engine test named for this card recomputes both on the Rust
port and holds each to its recorded value.

Interpretation. Ions feel each other over long distances, and the effect grows with charge squared. Water

column does discriminate between them.

Interpretation. The pressure of CO2 above the solvent. It is the single most important output of this

Derivation. Aronu Eq 10. Equate the fugacity of CO2 in each phase, with the liquid referred to Henry’s

Interpretation. The slope of the equilibrium curve gives the heat released when CO2 is absorbed. It

at 40 C and loadings 0.30 and 0.50, as a reading aid only.

Equation G.33: Table for column calls
Tier O
ln p∗(T, α) −2 ln α =
4
X
r=1
4
X
s=1
wr (T) ws (α) Λrs
(G.33)
Interpretation. A column calls p∗thousands of times, so the solved model is stored on a grid and read
back by interpolation.
Derivation. The grid holds 16 temperatures from 30 to 130 C and 40 loadings from 0.02 to 0.75, for one
MEA mass fraction, each node from a direct solve. Interpolation is cubic Lagrange in each direction.
At low loading p∗varies as α2, so the table stores the smooth remainder ln p∗−2 ln α and restores the
term afterwards. Every node still equals a direct solve.
Variables. wr, four-point Lagrange weight, 1; Λrs, stored value of ln p∗−2 ln α, 1.
Assumptions. Design strength of 30 mass%. Direct solve above that strength.
Source. [C] Calculated here.
Implementation. mea::pstar::stencil, inside the table built by mea::pstar::pstar_table
Validation. ID, test eq_g_33. Against Eq. G.19. Grid error below 1% is the stated target, and the
crate holds a tighter 0.5% gate at 30 mass%.
Worked example: Case B lean and rich solvent at 40 C
Take 1 kg of CO2-free solvent at 30 wt% MEA: 4.912 mol MEA and 38.86 mol water. At the lean
loading of 0.25 this carries 1.228 mol CO2, so the apparent mole fractions are 0.1092, 0.8636 and
0.0273. At 313.15 K, Eq. G.26 gives VMEA = 61.04 mL mol−1, and water at 992.2 kg m−3 gives 18.16
mL mol−1. Then Vsln = 22.17 mL mol−1, the mean molar mass is 23.43 g mol−1, the density is 1,057
kg m−3 and the MEA concentration is 4.92 kmol m−3. Eq. G.27 gives µsln/µw = 3.13. Eq. G.28 starts
from Dw = 2.71 × 10−9 m2 s−1 and gives Dl = 1.09 × 10−9 m2 s−1.
At the rich loading of 0.50 the same steps give 1,110 kg m−3, µsln/µw = 3.90 and Dl = 9.1 × 10−10
m2 s−1. Hartono Table 3 prints 1,059.8 kg m−3 at loading 0.29 and 1,098.7 at 0.50, so the Weiland
form runs 1.1% high at the rich end. These are hand figures for a loading range that Eq. G.32 allows.
The Case B plant moves 894 t h−1 of this solvent, on a CO2-free basis, by Eq. H.28.
Link to the sources
The prototype reproduced Aronu’s model column at 104 points only after the convention of Eq. G.7
was found, and it did so with activity expressions the library does not hold. That is strong indirect
support for [M7], and a copy of Thomsen and Rasmussen remains the first item to request. Against
measured data the same model gives an AARD of 21.9% on Aronu Table 4 and 8.4% on Dugas Table
4.4, which was outside Aronu’s regression. Both figures describe the prototype. At high pressure and
in hydrogen-rich gas, no local source holds equilibrium data for this solvent.
92

===== page 94
10
H. Rate-based MEA absorber
Rate-based here describes the method. The column is a one-dimensional film model with an approx-
imate enhancement factor, so these cards carry tier O, and it runs in a regime no local source has
measured: 30 wt% MEA on hydrogen-rich gas at 26.8 bar. The closest experiments use 20 wt% MEA
on methane at similar CO2 partial pressure. Each result from this section is therefore an estimate
with a stated test behind it, and none enters the headline ledger as a measured value.
Symbols keep this section apart from the rest of the document. E, E1 and E∞are enhancement factors
and M is the Hatta group, because E and M name stored energy and mass in Section A. Gn and
Ln are molar flows, because G is a mass velocity and L a tube length. ap is packing area and ae the
effective area that Sonderby writes as a. hGL, qGL and Sch replace plain h, q and S. Khan et al.
[15], Chem. Eng. Res. Des. 89 (2011) 1600, is cited as Khan, and Isa et al. [14], Int. J. Greenh. Gas
Control 56 (2017) 173, as Isa. The column model is in the engine, and each card names the function
that implements it. Each validation line prints the outcome that the validation results file records.
Gate M6 judged the column rows and failed, with 6 of 13 judged rows outside their limits (SA-1,
DICE-SRD, KW-SRD, MO-K16-TOUT, VI-3-SS2, VI-3-SS3). Terms T02 and T06 to T08 therefore
stay anchored in the Headline, and no modelled column value enters the ledger.
Equation H.1: Gas film
Tier O
NCO2 = kG (pCO2 −pint)
(H.1)
Interpretation. CO2 crosses a thin gas film by diffusion, driven by the drop in partial pressure from the
bulk gas to the interface.
Derivation. Whitman two-film theory, as Khan Fig 1 shows it. Each film resists in series and holds no
material at steady state.
Variables. NCO2, CO2 flux from gas to liquid, mol m−2 s−1; kG, gas film coeﬀicient, mol m−2 s−1 Pa−1;
pint, CO2 partial pressure at the interface, Pa.
Assumptions. Two-film.
Source. [P] Khan, Fig 1 and text, PDF p. 2.
Implementation. units::absorber::flux
Validation. ID, test eq_h_01. Sign and zero flux at zero driving force.

Equation H.7: Instantaneous enhancement
Tier O
E∞= 1 + CBDB
b DlCint
(H.7)
Interpretation. The ceiling on enhancement. Reaction can go no faster than MEA reaches the interface.
At high CO2 pressure the interface concentration is large, so this ceiling comes down.
Derivation. Khan Eq 4. With b = 2 from the carbamate reaction CO2 + 2RNH2, two MEA arrive for
each CO2. Khan gives no correlation for DB, so the caller supplies it.

Interpretation. Rate-side solubility of CO2 in the solvent. It belongs to the Khan set and stays with

Equation H.19: Gas temperature
Tier O
dTG
dz = −qGLaeAc
Gncp,G
(H.19)
Interpretation. The rising gas takes up heat from warmer liquid or gives it to cooler liquid. The sign
depends on where in the column it sits.

heat carried by the transferred species is charged to the liquid in Eq. H.20.

Equation H.20: Liquid temperature
Tier O
dTL
dz = (NCO2cp,CO2 + NH2Ocp,H2O) aeAc (TL −TG)
Lncp,L
−qGLaeAc
Lncp,L
+ (NCO2∆hCO2 + NH2O∆hH2O) aeAc
Lncp,L
(H.20)
Interpretation. Absorption releases heat into the liquid, and this drives the temperature bulge seen in
absorbers. The first term brings transferred gas to liquid temperature, and the second is exchange
with the gas.
Derivation. Liquid falls toward smaller z, so the balance across dz gives Lncp,L[TL(z) −TL(z + dz)] on
one side and the three heat inputs on the other. Signs follow the page image of Sonderby Eq 8. The
middle term is negative because heat from a hotter gas warms the falling liquid. The last term carries
enthalpy changes that are negative for exothermic absorption and condensation, so it also warms the
falling liquid, and ∆hCO2 = −∆habs from Eq. G.24. ∆hH2O is the condensation enthalpy of water.
Variables. TL, liquid temperature, K; cp,L, liquid molar heat capacity, J mol−1 K−1; ∆hCO2, enthalpy
change of CO2 absorption, J mol−1; ∆hH2O, enthalpy change of water condensation, J mol−1.
Assumptions. Signs from the image. Interface at the liquid temperature, assumption 2.
Source. [P] Sonderby et al. [29], Eq 8 and assumption 2, PDF p. 3, read as an image.

Tier O

Interpretation. Gas enters at the bottom and liquid at the top, so half the conditions sit at each end.

the eight conditions here.

judges the sign of the pressure trend only, so it is not counted as evidence on magnitude.

Equation H.30: Co-absorption
Tier F
˙nH2,abs = ˙nCH4,abs = ˙nCO,abs = 0
(H.30)
Interpretation. Hydrogen and methane also dissolve a little and leave with the solvent. No data exist
for this solvent, so the central case sets them to zero and states the reference value beside it.
Justification. No local source gives H2, CH4 or CO solubility in loaded MEA. The nearest reference is
activated MDEA in IEAGHG Case 1A. Flash gas stream 16 and CO2 stream 17 hold 2.80 kmol h−1
of H2 and 0.28 of CH4, against 4,989 and 201 in feed stream 7, or 0.056% and 0.137% [C]. Yield loss
is small on that reference. The effect on CO2 purity is larger, and it is disclosed.
Variables. ˙nH2,abs, H2 absorbed, mol s−1; ˙nCH4,abs, CH4 absorbed, mol s−1; ˙nCO,abs, CO absorbed,
mol s−1.
Assumptions. Zero central. Reference aMDEA, 0.056% H2 and 0.137% CH4.
Source. [C] Calculated here from IEAGHG 2017-02 [1], streams 7, 16 and 17, PDF p. 75 and p. 76.
[M10]: no solubility data for MEA.
Implementation. units::absorber::coabsorb
Validation. NONE, test eq_h_30. Disclosed term, with no benchmark for MEA.

===== page 106
Fit range and origin. Isa Eq 42, with kMEA = 4.61 × 109 exp(−4412/T) from Eq 43. Table 10 gives f
from 9.92 to 2.92 for the Tsai area and from 77.47 to 22.82 for the Billet area between 20 and 60 bar.
It was fitted to 20 wt% MEA with no regeneration, on CO2 and CH4 at 1:1, in one column. Moving
it to 30 wt% on hydrogen would have no basis, so the plant leaves it out.
