Worked example: Case B solvent, diameter and driving force
Solvent. Case B captures 48.3 t h−1 of CO2, or 305 mol s−1. With wMEA = 0.30 and a swing of 0.25,
Eq. H.28 gives 248.4 kg s−1, which is the 894 t h−1 of the ledger. The lean stream also carries its own
loading of 0.25, equal to the swing, so it adds another 48.3 t h−1 and weighs 942 t h−1.
Diameter. At the 1,057 kg m−3 of the Section G example that is 892 m3 h−1. Eq. H.29 then gives 3.4
m at a load of 100 and 5.3 m at a load of 40 m3 m−2 h−1. These sizes are for one column and use no
gas side check.
Driving force. The feed holds 17.7 mol% CO2 at 26.8 bar, so pCO2 = 4.74 bar at the bottom. With 4.9
kmol m−3 of MEA at 40 C, Eq. H.9 gives 30.8 atm L mol−1, or 3,117 Pa m3 mol−1, so Cint reaches
152 mol m−3 if the gas film is taken as negligible. Eq. H.5 gives k2 = 13,100 m3 kmol−1 s−1 at this
temperature. Eq. H.7 then gives E∞= 1 + 16 DB/Dl with total MEA as an upper bound on CB.
Khan gives no DB, and a ratio near 0.6 recovers the target of about 11. Free MEA at the rich end
is far smaller than the total, so the real ceiling there is lower still, which is the plain reason the rich
loading stays capped at 0.50.
Link to the sources
Sonderby supplies the column structure, tested at 30 wt% and 1 bar, and Khan the kinetic set with
a test at up to 7.24 bar. The high-pressure work of Isa and Salvinder uses 20 wt% MEA on natural
gas, and its findings warn against trust in any one correlation pair. So the absorber result carries a
named gap at every step, and the 90% per-pass capture stays an assumption of the ledger until the
column runs and its result is reported, whether it meets that figure or falls short.
11
I. MEA stripper
The stripper takes the loaded solvent back to its lean state and releases CO2 at low pressure. Heat is
the whole cost of this step, so the question here is how much reboiler duty each tonne of CO2 needs
and what grade of heat can supply it. The model is reduced order: at 120 C reactions are fast, so
the column is treated as equilibrium limited. The specific reboiler duty (SRD) of 3.8 GJ t−1 stays
the ledger input. The stripper and regeneration train are in the engine, and gate M6 judged their
benchmark rows and failed, so the input stays anchored. Eq. I.13 is an independent bottom-up check,
and it never replaces the input without a benchmark on syngas, which does not exist in the library
[M12]. All worked numbers are at Case B unless the card says otherwise.
Equation I.1: CO2 balance
Tier R
˙nCO2 = ˙nMEA (αrich −αlean)
(I.1)

===== page 107
Interpretation. The CO2 released equals the loading the solvent gives up on one pass.
The cyclic
capacity αrich −αlean therefore sets how much solvent must circulate.
Derivation. Eq. A.3 for CO2 on the column at steady state, with MEA conserved:
˙nMEAαrich =
˙nMEAαlean + ˙nCO2.
Variables. ˙nCO2, CO2 released, mol s−1; ˙nMEA, MEA circulation, mol s−1.
Assumptions. SS. No solvent degradation.
Source. [T] Consequence of Eq. A.3.

Equation I.2: Desorption driving force
Tier O
NCO2 = KG (p∗−pCO2)
(I.2)
Interpretation. Absorption run in reverse. CO2 leaves the liquid while the equilibrium pressure over
the solvent exceeds the pressure in the gas.
Derivation. Eq. H.4 with the sign reversed, so that a positive flux leaves the liquid. The form is kept
only to scope the claim made in Eq. I.3. By Eq. H.5 the second-order rate constant rises by a factor
of 25 between 40 and 120 C, which is why this document stops short of integrating a rate model here.
Variables. NCO2, CO2 flux, positive out of the liquid, mol m−2 s−1; KG, overall gas-phase coeﬀicient,
mol m−2 s−1 Pa−1; p∗, equilibrium CO2 pressure, Pa; pCO2, CO2 partial pressure in the gas, Pa.
Assumptions. Equilibrium limited. This equation is not integrated.
Source. [T] Reverse of Eq. H.4.
Implementation. units::stripper::desorb
Validation. Scoped, test eq_i_02. No separate benchmark. The stage model in Eq. I.3 carries the test.
Equation I.3: Equilibrium stage
Tier O
yCO2,k p = p∗
CO2 (Tk, αk) ,
k = 1, . . . , N
(I.3)
Interpretation. Each of N stages brings vapour and liquid to equilibrium. This suits a hot, fast system,
where the column is limited by how much steam reaches each height and hardly at all by reaction
rate.
Derivation. Set the flux of Eq. I.2 to its limit of zero driving force on each stage, then link stages by
Eq. I.1 and an enthalpy balance. Equilibrium pressures come from Section G. The kinetic expressions
in Plaza are quoted for reference and are not used.
Variables. yCO2,k, CO2 mole fraction on stage k, 1; p∗
CO2, equilibrium CO2 pressure over the solvent,
Pa; Tk, stage temperature, K; αk, stage loading, mol mol−1; N, number of equilibrium stages, 1.
Assumptions. Fast reaction at 120 C. Stage eﬀiciency of one.
Source. [P] Plaza [24], Eq 3.46, 3.47, PDF p. 98, kinetics quoted and unused. Cownden and Lucquiaud
[5], PDF p. 11, compare regenerator temperature profiles with pilot data.

Tier O

˙nCO2
= p −p∗

p∗
CO2
(I.4)
Interpretation. Water leaving the top of the column carries heat that the condenser throws away. The

Derivation. The overhead vapour leaves close to equilibrium with the rich feed, so yCO2 = p∗

Equation I.13: SRD split
Tier O
SRD = qdes + qsens + qstrip,
qdes = ∆habs
MCO2
(I.13)
Interpretation. The first term breaks the chemical bond and is set by the solvent. The other two,
warming the solvent over the last few kelvin and raising steam that the condenser later removes,
respond to design choices.
Derivation. Split Eq. I.6 by where the enthalpy goes. The first term is the heat of absorption per unit
mass of CO2. The second is ˙mrcp∆Thx/ ˙mCO2 from Eq. I.10. The third is ˙Qcond/ ˙mCO2 from Eq. I.7.

of 3.64 leaves qdes = 1.94 GJ t−1. The desorption term is back-calculated, so this split closes by
construction and tests nothing about it. At Case B, qsens = 0.70 and with the same two other terms
the bottom-up total is 3.61 GJ t−1. It stays a check on flue-gas terms.

Interpretation. The value the ledger actually uses. It sits above the bottom-up figure and close to the

Equation I.17: Source temperature
Tier F
Tsource ≥Treb + ∆Tmin
(I.17)
Interpretation. Heat must arrive hotter than the reboiler. This line decides which plant streams count
as reboiler heat, and it rules out most low-grade syngas heat.
Justification. Second law, with a minimum approach. DICE heats its kettle with 60 psia saturated
steam, which is 145 C by the Antoine fit in the engine inputs. INL distributes LP steam at 307 F and
75 psia, which is 153 C. With a reboiler at 121.9 C those set the working band, and syngas counts
only above 130 C.
Variables. Tsource, heating medium temperature, K; ∆Tmin, minimum approach, K.
Assumptions. Steam near 145 to 153 C.
Source. [P] DICE [22], PDF p. 186. TEV-961 [36], A-57, PDF p. 81, read as an image.
Implementation. plant::heatrec::check_grade
Validation. PUB, test eq_i_17. Grade rule only. The stack in Eq. J.17 counts syngas above 130 C and
nothing colder.

===== page 113
Equation I.18: Interheating, listed and not carried
Tier F
SRDtot = SRDreb + qIH
(I.18)
Interpretation. A stripper interheater moves duty out of the reboiler to a cooler source. The reboiler
figure falls and the total heat barely changes.
Justification. Vinjarapu report 2.71 GJ t−1 on the reboiler with 28% interheating, against 3.71 in the
base case. Adding the interheater back at 53 kJ kg−1 on 264 kg h−1 of solvent and 13.1 kg h−1 of
CO2 gives 1.07, for a total of 3.78 GJ t−1. So 2.71 is a reboiler figure, and it is never quoted here as
a saving.
Variables. SRDtot, total stripping heat per unit CO2, J kg−1; SRDreb, reboiler share, J kg−1; qIH,
interheater share, J kg−1.
Assumptions. Not in the design.
Source. [P] Vinjarapu et al., Energy 320 (2025) 134973, Table 4, PDF p. 17. [C] for the sum.
Implementation. none. No code path.
Validation. NONE, test eq_i_18. Not carried, because total heat is unchanged and the plant has no
surplus cool source above the band in Eq. I.17.
Worked example: regeneration at Case B
Start from 1097.90 kmol h−1 captured, which is 48.3 t h−1. Eq. I.1 with a capacity of 0.25 gives 4,392
kmol h−1 of MEA, and Eq. I.9 turns that into 894 t h−1 of solvent at 30 wt%. Eq. I.12 at 3.8 GJ t−1
gives a reboiler duty of 51.0 MW. Dividing by the rich solvent flow gives 195 kJ kg−1 by Eq. I.15,
which sits just under the pilot optimum. The plant steam stack of Eq. J.17 covers 37.9 MW of this,
and the other 13.1 MW falls to the reactor.
Link to the sources
The flue-gas pilots agree on a narrow band of SRD, and the DICE design shows how that heat divides.
What does not transfer cleanly is the absorber side: syngas at 26.8 bar reaches its rich loading with
little pinch, yet the stripper still works at 2 bar, so the desorption term is unchanged. That is why
the carried value stays near flue-gas practice, and why [M12] remains open.
12
J. Integrated plant
This section joins the unit models into one plant and asks how large the reactor must be. The order of
work matters: scale the INL reference first, then close the carbon balance, then the heat and electricity
balances, and only then report. INL Case 5 stands in for Case 6 on every syngas duty because the
Case 6 sheets are not in the library [M1], scaled by 1,927 over 1,970 where used.
The reported terms map to cards as follows: reactor heat Eq. J.26; IHX duty and loss Eq. C.1 and
Eq. B.15; process heat Eq. J.14; shift heat Eq. E.6; syngas recovery Eq. F.2; regeneration Eq. J.16;
steam generation Eq. J.17; electricity Eq. B.18; helium Eq. B.12; pumping and compression Eq. J.20
and Eq. J.22; cooling Eq. J.31; unmet heat Eq. J.18; unmet electricity Eq. J.29. Values here are
the Headline preset of the final results: the anchored port with term T05, syngas heat above 130 C,
modelled, because gate M4 passed for it. Gates M6 and M7 failed, so the column and flowsheet terms
stay anchored and their modelled values appear only in the drift table of the results. The comparison
with the screening values (GOLD) runs on the Anchored preset, so a Headline value differs from its
screening value by the T05 term alone.

Equation J.8: Tube carbon
Tier O
TC =
FC −frP
1 −fr (1 −ηppϕc)
(J.8)
Interpretation. MEA lets 10% of the CO2 slip, so more carbon returns through the recycle than INL
sent back. The tubes then carry more carbon than INL’s Selexol plant at the same feed.
Derivation. Start from TC = FC + frG. Tail gas is G = TC −C −P and capture is C = ηppϕcTC, so
G = TC(1 −ηppϕc) −P. Substituting and collecting TC gives the statement.
Variables. G, tail-gas carbon, mol s−1; C, carbon captured, mol s−1; ηpp, per-pass capture, 1.
Assumptions. Slip passes the cryogenic unit with the INL split, which INL did not model.

Equation J.17: Heat stack for the reboiler
Tier O
˙Qstack = ˙QSel + ˙Qexp + ˙Qsyn
(J.17)
Interpretation. Three plant streams can serve the reboiler before the reactor is asked. They are spent

Derivation. Removing Selexol frees its LP steam, 65.0 MMBtu h−1 in Case 5. Export steam is the
83.7 MMBtu h−1 of Case 6. Syngas above 130 C gives 11.5 MW in Case 5. Case 5 duties are scaled
by 1,927 over 1,970, and all three by s. Results match the screening order whenever the whole stack
is used, which holds at all central cases.

syngas heat above 130 C, W.

Equation J.36: Rated against required
Tier O
˙Qspare = ˙Qrated −˙QR

Worked example: closing Case B
Carbon first. Eq. J.5 gives 1219.23 kmol h−1 of feed carbon. With fr = 0.752, ηpp = 0.90 and ϕc =
0.775, Eq. J.8 gives 1574.06 in the tubes, Eq. J.9 gives 1097.90 captured and Eq. J.10 leaves 116.90 in
the stack. Eq. J.11 closes with 4.42 in the product.
Heat next. Process heat is 120.80 MW, with 0.88 of slip heat. The reboiler wants 51.0 MW, and the
stack gives 37.9, so the gap is 13.12. Electricity totals 19.78 MWe, of which 7.45 runs the circulators.
Eq. J.26 then converges at 178.49 MWth, leaving 31.5 MWth of the 210 rating unused.
Link to the sources
INL’s 925 C case reaches 178.8 MWt on heat and power (TEV-961, Table 2, PDF p. 18). That figure
is close to the 178.5 here at a smaller plant only by coincidence: this design scales down by 0.6833
and then adds a reboiler gap, the restored purge row, losses and circulators that INL did not carry.
The walk in Eq. J.35 shows each step.
13
K. Emissions
This section turns the carbon balance into tonnes per year and asks how much CO2 the plant keeps out
of the atmosphere. The headline basis is direct emissions to atmosphere against a same-size unabated
SMR, a new-build at SOXAL scale, using the INL baseline. Per-pass and plant capture are shown
together, and lifecycle stays a sensitivity. The PSA stays external, and its reject carbon is still counted,
so the boundary cannot hide carbon. The compressor and offshore storage sit inside the dashed line
of Figure 2. Eq. K.17 explains this, and the accounting of carbon stored outside Singapore remains
open.

===== page 127

Equation K.17: Tail-gas fates

Afate = Ebase −MCO2th ( ˙nburn + ˙nexp)
(K.17)
Interpretation. Carbon in the PSA reject has three possible fates: recycle, burn on site or export.

burned.

boundary, since ˙nburn is then zero, yet its carbon still reaches the air. That is why the headline counts
the atmosphere. Compressor and offshore storage follow Figure 2 and sit inside the line. Whether
carbon stored outside Singapore counts as Singapore abatement is an open question and is not settled
here.

Tier O
ALC = A + Eup −Enuc −ETS
(K.19)
Interpretation. A sensitivity only. Saved gas also saves its upstream emissions, while the nuclear chain
and storage have small burdens of their own.
Derivation. Upstream gas at 11.5 kg GJ−1 on the gas saved, nuclear at 5.5 kg MWh−1 on electricity-
equivalent output at the charged rating, and transport and storage at 2.5% of the tonnes stored.
131

===== page 133
Variables. ALC, lifecycle abatement, t y−1; Eup, saved upstream gas emissions, t y−1; Enuc, nuclear
lifecycle burden, t y−1; ETS, transport and storage burden, t y−1.
Assumptions. Sensitivity only. The factors were not re-read at source.
Source. [C] This document. Factors as carried in the project inputs.
Implementation. plant::emissions::Model::lifecycle_terms
Validation. GOLD, test eq_k_19. 0.616 Mt y−1 with saved gas counted, 0.566 with burdens only.
Equation K.20: Backup firing
Tier O
ebk = esite +
˙QprocEF
ηbk ˙mH2
(K.20)
Interpretation. Central hydrogen runs only in the 85% availability hours. If the plant kept running
through reactor outages, it would burn gas for process heat, and the intensity of those hours would
rise sharply.
Derivation. Site intensity plus fired heat per kilogram of H2 at boiler eﬀiciency ηbk and emission factor
EF. Computed here: esite = 0.572 and the fired term is 3.19, so ebk = 3.77 kg kg−1, or 44,476 t y−1
on the extra H2. The engine runs this card as its heater route: 44,476 t y−1 emitted and 64,293 t y−1
of added abatement. It also runs two conventional-train routes at INL’s rates: 29,017 t y−1 emitted
with capture, adding 79,753, and 108,770 unabated, adding 0. Over 1,314 h y−1 every route adds
11,810 t y−1 of H2. Furnace and heater capital are unpriced.
Variables. ebk, intensity in backup hours, kg kg−1; esite, site intensity, kg kg−1; EF, combustion emis-
sion factor, kg J−1; ηbk, backup firing eﬀiciency, 0.85, 1.
Assumptions. Sensitivity only, by project decision. ηbk = 0.85 assumed.
Source. [M29]. The need for backup is stated in IAEA NP-T-4.3, PDF p. 23.
Implementation. plant::emissions::Model::backup_heater, with the conventional-train routes in
plant::emissions::Model::backup
Validation. NONE, test eq_k_20. No benchmark. The hand route above is an independent check on
the engine heater route.

Equation K.22: Target test
Tier R
A > 0.25 Mt y−1
(K.22)
Interpretation. The project statement’s abatement requirement.
Derivation. Compare Eq. K.11 with the target.
Variables. A, direct abatement, t y−1.
Assumptions. Direct abatement.
Source. [A] Project statement.
Implementation. plant::export::results_block, which writes the row of abatement above the target
Validation. ID, test eq_k_22. 0.578 Mt y−1 at Case B, 328,052 t y−1 above the target. The lowest
mass-balanced case is burn on site with gas held, at 0.357 Mt y−1.
Worked example: abatement at Case B
Eq. K.1 gives 9.21 kg kg−1, and 66,925 t y−1 of hydrogen makes the baseline 616,361 t y−1 by Eq. K.3.
The plant stores 359,777 t y−1 and emits 38,309 t y−1 from the nitrogen-rich cut. The rest of the
baseline never forms as CO2 because reactor heat replaces the furnace and recycle cuts the gas bought.
Eq. K.11 gives 578,052 t y−1, or 0.578 Mt y−1, with per-pass capture 0.90 and plant capture 0.9005.
Link to the sources
INL published a plant emitting 142 st d−1 at Case 6 (TEV-961, Table 2, PDF p. 17). Scaled, that is
below the residual here, because MEA slip raises the burned cut above the Selexol case. The difference
is the price of swapping a physical solvent for MEA, and it is the reason Eq. J.8 carries the recycle
explicitly.
14
L. Economics
This section prices the plant by two independent methods that feed one result.
Method 1, annual incremental ledger. The ladder L0 to L5 lists annual cost differences against
a same-size unabated SMR, with capital annualised. Eq. L.1 to Eq. L.33 build it. Method 1 answers
the question “what does each item add?”
Method 2, discounted cash flow. Eq. L.34 to Eq. L.37 run year-by-year real cash flows at 8% for
two plants of equal hydrogen output on a common horizon, the unabated SMR and NUS-H2. Method
2 answers the question “what does each plant cost, and what does the difference buy?”
The central ledger is Case B with recycle, ns = 1.0 with 0.6 and 0.31 at equal prominence, bottom-up
capture and T&S at S$60/t. Prices are in 2025 S$. The headline is the full cost set, which is L5 and
the DCF, with the reactor charged at 210 MWth at BOAK and FOAK, at 25 y and 60 y. L3 prints
beneath it as a named step. Carbon tax stays out of the headline. The S$100/t target is tested at the
end of the section, and no claim is made unless a computed central case passes.
Method 1: annual incremental ledger
Equation L.1: Capital recovery factor
Tier R
CRF =
i
1 −(1 + i)−tℓ
(L.1)
Interpretation. The level annual payment that repays one dollar of capital over the life, with interest.
It works like a mortgage payment.
133

===== page 135
Derivation. A unit payment at each year end has present value Ptℓ
y=1(1 + i)−y = [1 −(1 + i)−tℓ]/i,
a geometric sum. Setting that annuity times the payment equal to the capital and solving for the
payment gives the factor.
Variables. CRF, capital recovery factor, y−1; i, real discount rate, 1; tℓ, life or horizon, y; y, year
index, y.
Assumptions. End-year payments.
Source. [T] Standard annuity result.
Implementation. plant::anchored::crf
Validation. PUB, test eq_l_01. Computed here at 8%: 0.09368, 0.08386 and 0.08080 at 25, 40 and 60
y. The results row SCREENING_CRF_25 records the first.
Equation L.2: Annualised capital
Tier R
Cann = C CRF
(L.2)
Interpretation. Turns a lump of capital into a yearly cost that can sit beside fuel and labour.
Derivation. Definition from Eq. L.1.
Variables. Cann, annualised capital, S$ y−1; C, capital, S$.
Assumptions. Constant real payment.
Source. [T] Standard annuity result.
Implementation. plant::economics::Model::ledger
Validation. ID, test eq_l_02. Unit identity.
Equation L.3: Sinking fund factor
Tier R
SFF =
i
(1 + i)tℓ−1
(L.3)
Interpretation. The yearly deposit that grows to one dollar at the end of the life. Long lives make it
tiny, which is why decommissioning weighs so little at 8%.
Derivation. Deposits at year ends grow to [(1 + i)tℓ−1]/i by the horizon, a geometric sum. Inverting
gives the factor. It equals CRF (1 + i)−tℓ.
Variables. SFF, sinking fund factor, y−1.
Assumptions. End-year deposits at the discount rate.
Source. [T] Standard annuity result.
Implementation. plant::anchored::sff
Validation. GOLD, test eq_l_03. Computed here: 0.01368 at 25 y and 0.00080 at 60 y.
Equation L.4: Interest during construction
Tier R
fIDC = (1 + i)tc −1
i tc
(L.4)
Interpretation. Money spent during a long build sits idle until the plant runs. This factor says how
much the overnight cost grows by the first day of operation.
Derivation. Equal spends at year ends reach (C/tc)[(1 + i)tc −1]/i at completion, the future value of
an annuity. Dividing by C gives the factor. It is applied at a non-integer tc as a smooth extension,
which is one source of the gap in Eq. L.37.
134

===== page 136
Variables. fIDC, interest during construction factor, 1; tc, construction time, 5.5, y.
Assumptions. Uniform spend, 5.5 y base case.
Source. [P] Construction time from INL/RPT-23-74378 (NZW09), Table ES-1, PDF p. 7. [C] for the
factor.
Implementation. plant::economics::Model::idc_factor
Validation. GOLD, test eq_l_04. Computed here: 1.198 at 8% and 5.5 y.
Equation L.5: Overnight capital, no turbine
Tier R
ckWt = ckWe (1 −0.09) · 0.40
(L.5)
Interpretation. INL quotes reactors per electrical kilowatt. A heat-only plant buys no turbine, so that
share comes off, and 40% eﬀiciency converts to thermal kilowatts.
Derivation. INL’s worked example for a non-electric HTGR, steps 3 and 4. It uses low and high OCC
of 4,000 and 7,000 US$2019 kWe−1. The project applies the same recipe to the 6,000 medium value of
Table 1.
Variables. ckWt, overnight capital cost, no turbine, US$2019 kWt−1; ckWe, BOAK overnight capital
cost, US$2019 kWe−1.
Assumptions. Turbine share 9%, eﬀiciency 40%.
Source. [P] INL/RPT-23-72972 Rev 3, PDF p. 47 and Table 1, PDF p. 5. [C] for the central value.
Implementation. plant::economics::Model::reactor_capex, on the overnight cost per kWt of the
registry
Validation. PUB, test eq_l_05. INL prints US$1,456 and US$2,548 per kWt. The recipe gives US$2,184
at the medium value.
Equation L.6: Reactor capital
Tier O
CR = ckWt ˙QrefF19mstage
 ˙Qrated
˙Qref
!ns
(L.6)
Interpretation. Cost per kilowatt at a reference size, re-based to 2025 S$, adjusted for build stage, then
scaled to the rating. With ns = 1 the reference size drops out and the cost is linear in rating.
Derivation. Reference cost is ckWt ˙Qref. Scaling by a power law in size gives the bracket. INL’s survey
found no significant difference in specific cost between large reactors and SMRs, which is the basis for
ns = 1 as central.

Equation L.8: Learning from BOAK
Tier O
mstage = 0.9log2 10 = 0.7047
(L.8)
Interpretation. A 10% cost fall per doubling, carried to the tenth unit from the BOAK cost.
Derivation. INL’s multi-unit example applies BOAK ×(1−0.1)log2 N to second and third units. Taken
to N = 10 this gives the multiplier.
Variables. N, unit number, 10, 1.
Assumptions. Printed as a route, never as a headline.

(L.23)
Interpretation. Recycling tail gas needs compressors and a cryogenic unit that no INL table prices. A
compression cost per megawatt stands in for them.
Fit range and origin. IEAGHG Case 1A prices CO2 compression at EUR 17.0M total plant cost. Over
INL’s 3.32 kW per st d−1 at 46.6 t h−1, that is a cost per MW. Applied to 5.7 MWe, with TCR over
TPC of 1.3 and re-based at 2.322, this gives S$71.5M at Case A and S$48.8M at Case B. The fit range
is one proxy, and the high case uses IEAGHG’s cryogenic block.
Variables. Crec, recycle train capital, S$; Crec,A, recycle train capital at Case A, S$; Cann,rec, recycle
train annual line, S$ y−1.
Assumptions. No published cost [M31].
Source. [C] From IEAGHG 2017-02 [1], PDF p. 223 for the compression cost, as carried in the project
inputs.
Implementation. plant::economics::Model::cost_items,
line RECYCLE_TRAIN of plant::
economics::Model::ledger

Interpretation. Each rung adds one group of real costs, so the reader sees exactly what moves the result.
Derivation. The rungs are fixed: L0 gas, reactor capital, capture capital, capture fixed O&M and T&S.

power island and the recycle train. L5 adds lost steam, baseline power and any export revenue.

Equation L.28: Levelised form of the ledger
Tier O
LAC = NPV (∆C)
NPV (A)
(L.28)
Interpretation. Cost and tonnes are both spread over time, so both are discounted. Method 1 is the
special case in which every year looks the same.
Derivation. If ∆C and A are constant from year 1 to tℓ, each NPV is the constant times the same
annuity sum, the sum cancels, and LAC = AC of Eq. L.26. Method 1 reaches that form by annualising
capital. Method 2 keeps the true timing, and Eq. L.36 evaluates it year by year.
Variables. LAC, levelised abatement cost, S$ t−1; NPV, net present value, S$.
Assumptions. Tonnes discounted at the same rate as cost.
Source. [C] This document.

Equation L.31: Levers to the target
Tier O
AC (q∗) = 100 S$ t−1
(L.31)
Interpretation. How far one input alone would have to move to reach the target. A lever that lands
outside any credible range shows that the target cannot be met on that input alone.
Derivation. The ceiling is 100A = S$57.8M per year. The ledger is linear in gas price, T&S and reactor
OCC, so each lever solves directly from the excess over the ceiling.
Variables. q∗, value of an input that meets the target, various.
Assumptions. One lever at a time, stated per lever.

Equation L.32: SMR plus CCS comparator
Tier O
ACcmp = F14CAC3 + (pTS −pTS,3) ecap,3
eav,3
(L.32)
Interpretation. What a fired SMR with MEA on its flue gas costs per tonne avoided on the same tariff,
one labelled row for comparison.
Derivation. IEAGHG Case 3 prints a CO2 avoidance cost of EUR 69.8/t with T&S at EUR 10/t.

over avoided CO2, 0.8004 over 0.7203 kg Nm−3. Gas price and availability differ from this ledger, so
the row is indicative. An earlier lifecycle comparator of this project sits on a different basis and is not
carried.

Equation L.33: Guards
Tier F
F19 = 1.6195,
F14 = 2.322,
Ecap energy counted once
(L.33)
Interpretation. Rules that keep the ledger honest. Capture energy is paid in heat and power, so it is
never priced a second time. Every price is carried to one base year, and carbon tax stays outside the
ledger.
Justification. F19 is the GDP deflator ratio 128.885 over 103.9745 times 1.3065. F14 is the corresponding
EUR2014 factor from the project inputs. A GDP deflator understates nuclear escalation, so reactor
prices err low. Bottom-up capture carries capital and fixed O&M only. Carbon tax credits sit outside
every line.
Variables. F19, US$2019 to S$2025 factor, S$ US$−1.
Assumptions. GDP deflator.
Source. [C] Project inputs.
Implementation. The factors are registry entries read by plant::anchored::Model::new. No single
guard function exists.

Equation L.34: Discounted cash flow
Tier R
NPVx =
X
j
X
y
cj,x (y) (1 + i)−y
crep
j
(y) =
(
Cj
y = 25m < tℓ
−Cj u/25
y = tℓ
,
u = 25
 tℓ
25

−tℓ
(L.34)
Interpretation. Each plant is a list of dated bills. Discounting brings every bill to year 0 and adds
them. Items with a 25 y life are bought again on schedule, and the part of the last unit still unused
at the horizon comes back as a straight-line credit.
Derivation. The sum is the definition of present value at real rate i. Reactor capital spreads uniformly
over tc, a part year first as the earliest bucket: CR · 0.5/5.5 at year −5 and CR/5.5 at years −4 to
0. The SMR island enters at year 0 at Total Capital Requirement, because TCR already holds the
island’s own interest during construction, so spreading it would count that interest twice. IEAGHG’s
TCR is EUR 222.89M and its fixed O&M EUR 7,545,514 per year. Raw water and chemicals with
catalysts, EUR 99,365 and EUR 420,000 per year, are stated at 95% availability and scale to 85%.
Re-based at 2.322 these are S$517.6M, S$17.52M per year and S$1.08M per year. Its feedstock, fuel
and electricity lines are left out because the gas, steam and power lines cover them. Both plants
carry the island unchanged, on the assumption that reformer differences cancel. Replacement runs at
m = 0, 1, . . . while 25m < tℓ, so at 60 y units are bought at years 0, 25 and 50, and 15 of the last 25
y are unused, which gives a credit of 0.6Cj at year 60. Nothing is bought at the horizon itself.
Variables. NPVx, present cost of plant x, S$; cj,x, cash flow of line j on plant x, S$ y−1; crep
j
, purchase
or credit of a replaced item, S$; Cj, capital of item j, S$; u, unused years of the last unit at the
horizon, y; m, replacement index, 1.
Assumptions. Real flows at 8%. Whole-year horizon. Costs from the same set as Method 1. Replaced
lines: SMR island, capture, IHX, recycle train, each on a 25 y life. At the high cost level only, the
IHX life is its rated hours over the operating hours, cut to whole years.
Source. [P] IEAGHG 2017-02 [1], Table 3, PDF p. 17, and Table 4, PDF p. 18. INL/RPT-23-74378
(NZW09), Table ES-1, PDF p. 7, for 5.5 y. [C] for the method.

Equation L.36: Levelised abatement cost
Tier R
LAC = NPVNUS −NPVSMR
A Ptℓ
y=1 (1 + i)−y
(L.36)
Interpretation. The present cost difference between the two plants, spread over present tonnes abated.
The SMR island appears on both sides and cancels, so only the new items and the gas, steam and
power differences remain.
Derivation. Eq. L.28 with NPV(∆C) = NPVNUS −NPVSMR and constant annual tonnes. Dividing
through by MH2 and the annuity gives LAC = (LCOHNUS −LCOHSMR) MH2/A, a useful check.

Equation L.37: Gap test between the methods
Tier R
g = LAC −ACL5
ACL5
,
|g| ≤0.10

j Cj (Θ CRF −CRF25)

(L.37)
Interpretation. Both methods price the same items, so they must nearly agree. Any difference has to be
a named timing effect. A gap above 10% must be explained by a named term in the documentation,
147

===== page 149
and it is never tuned away. The test checks the bookkeeping, and an input wrong in both methods
would pass it.
Derivation. Annual lines match exactly, because they enter both methods unchanged. Decommissioning
matches too: D(1 + i)−tℓspread by CRF equals D SFF, by the note in Eq. L.3. Licence and power
island at year 0 match by Eq. L.1. Two terms remain. Reactor timing: the discrete spend gives
fs = [0.5(1 + i)5 + P4
k=0(1 + i)k]/5.5 = 1.2002, against the smooth Eq. L.4, 1.198. Replacement: the
sum over j runs over capture, IHX and recycle train, with Θ = P
m(1 + i)−25m −(u/25)(1 + i)−tℓ,
which is 1 at 25 y and 1.1614 at 60 y. The SMR island nets out of the difference.
Variables. g, relative gap between methods, 1; ACL5, ledger abatement cost at L5, S$ t−1; fs, spend
factor of the discrete schedule, 1; Θ, present worth of replacements per unit capital, 1; CRF25, capital
recovery factor at 25 y, y−1.

The results count 0 of 8 central cases at or below S$100/t, on L5 and the DCF, at BOAK and FOAK,
at 25 y and 60 y. The cost target is therefore missed on this basis, and no S$100/t claim is made.
BOAK at 25 y exceeds the ceiling of S$57.8M per year by S$51.9M per year on the full cost set, and
by S$21.8M per year at L3. Charging only the 178.5 MWth the process needs brings L3 to 112.7
and 95.1 at 25 and 60 y, and L5 to 161.2 and 140.4. The abatement target is met by a wide margin,
Eq. K.22.
Link to the sources
INL’s cost survey gives quartiles rather than a price for a 925 C reactor, and it warns against reading
more precision into them than they hold. The result here inherits that spread: the size exponent and
stage move the abatement cost by more than any plant design choice in Sections I to K. The DCF
confirms that the ledger’s arithmetic is sound, and what stays uncertain is the reactor price itself.
Items carried on proxies, [M23] and [M31], are named on their cards.

===== page 154
Differences from the screening model
The engine reproduces the earlier screening model wherever the two share a method. Reference tests
against that model expect the five differences below, and a test that misses by more than the listed
effect points to a porting error in the code.
1. Duct loss. Eq. B.14 scales the loss with
√
˙m, where the screening model scales it with s: 0.37
against 0.31 MW at Case B.
2. Reboiler steam order. The screening model draws freed Selexol steam, then EX4 and ZLD
steam, then export steam. This document follows the project order: freed Selexol steam, export
steam, syngas above 130 C, then reactor steam. The two agree whenever the reboiler uses the
whole stack, which holds in every central case. They part only when the reboiler needs less than
the stack, for example at an SRD of 3.4 with ZLD steam freed, and then the export steam kept
and the lost steam change.
3. Water saturation. Saturation in Eq. F.11 comes from IAPWS-IF97, so the result differs slightly
from the Antoine value of the screening model.
4. Size exponent. The default here is ns = 1.0, against 0.6 in the screening model.
5. Reformer duty. The species-enthalpy duty of Eq. D.34 sits below the INL heat basis. The ledger
keeps Eq. J.14 and prints both.
